<div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-7"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="7" data-folio="1" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n6_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 1">[p. 1]</button></div><h2 class="sec-h1">A JOURNEY FROM MADRAS, &amp;c.</h2><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 15. Mr. Ravenshaw’s answers to my queries.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">BEFORE I proceed to give an account of my journey through the province of <em>Canara</em>, I shall prepare my reader, by detailing the answers which were sent to my queries by Mr. Ravenshaw, the collector of the southern division; a young gentleman who does credit to the school of Colonel Read, and to Mr. Hurdis, under whom he was formed to business.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Query</em> 1st. What proportion of your district consists of land that has always been uncultivated? Of this, what part might, with proper management, be converted into rice-ground? what part into coco-nut or <em>Betel-nut</em> gardens? What proportion of this waste land is now cleared for grass, what is under forest, and what is enclosed for plantations of timber trees, firewood, &amp;c.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 15.</aside></div><p class="prose-p"><em>Answer.</em> No account of the extent of jungles (forests) has ever been taken. All the surveys that have been made only went to ascertain the cultivated lands, and those capable of culture, but not at present cultivated, and which are 111,965½ <em>Morays</em>. Of this, 24,181 <em>Morays</em> are cleared for grass, 7,043 have a capability of being converted into rice ground, and 1,789 are fit for gardens. No <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-8" data-page="8" data-folio="2" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n7_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 2">[p. 2]</button> account is kept of the quantity enclosed for timber, but all the remainder would answer for the purpose. N. B. The average <em>Moray</em> is 45 <em>Guntas</em>, each 33 feet square, or 49,005 square feet, and is therefore nearly 1 <sup>13</sup>⁄<sub>100</sub> acre.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Q.</em> 2d. What proportion of your district consists of rice-land? Of this, what proportion has been cultivated last year, what has been waste or unoccupied?</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>A.</em> 247,218 <em>Morays</em>; of which 225,782 were cultivated, and the remainder was waste, owing to a want of tenants. Of that which was cultivated, 1,591 <em>Morays</em> were overflowed, and the crops destroyed.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Q.</em> 3d. What proportion of your district consists of garden grounds? In these, how many coco-nut or <em>Betel-nut</em> trees, and trees for supporting pepper vines, are planted? Is the estimate of these founded on any recent survey, or from an old valuation?</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>A.</em> The number of trees contained in the gardens, according to the public accompts, are, coco-nut 695,060, <em>Betel-nut</em> 1,155,850, <em>Mangos</em> 59,772, sundries 54,362, pepper vines 368,828. This estimate is formed from an old survey made in the year 179 <sup>2</sup>⁄<sub>3</sub>. The number of trees, of each description, is at least double of what is here mentioned.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Q.</em> 4th. How many ploughs are there in your district?</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>A.</em> 71,716.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Q.</em> 5th. How many slaves of all ages, and both sexes?</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>A.</em> 7924.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Q.</em> 6th. How many houses?</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>A.</em> 71,856.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Q.</em> 7th. Of these, how many are inhabited by Christians?</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>A.</em> 2,545.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Q.</em> 8th. How many by Mussulmans, including <em>Moplays</em>?</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>A.</em> 5,223.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Q.</em> 9th. How many by <em>Bráhmans</em>, including <em>Namburis</em>?</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-9"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="9" data-folio="3" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n8_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 3">[p. 3]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 15.</aside></div><p class="prose-p"><em>A.</em> 7,187, exclusive of <em>Kankánies</em>, the <em>Bráhmans</em> of which nation are confounded with the other casts.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Q.</em> 10th. How many by <em>Jain</em>?</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>A.</em> 2,700.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Q.</em> 11th. How many by those who wear the <em>Lingam</em>?</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>A.</em> 880.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Q.</em> 12th. How many by <em>Nairs</em>?</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>A.</em> 788.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Q.</em> 13th. How many by <em>Massady Buntars</em>?</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>A.</em> 7,123.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Q.</em> 14th. How many by <em>Jain Buntars</em>?</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>A.</em> 1,060.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Q.</em> 15th. How many by <em>Kankánies</em>?</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>A.</em> 2,434.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Q.</em> 16th. How many animals of the cow kind are there in your district?</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>A.</em> Cows 62,130, males 98,860, calves 59,109.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Q.</em> 17th. How many animals of the buffalo kind?</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>A.</em> Females 12,129, males 43,596, calves 6,882.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Q.</em> 18th. What quantity of seed rice is sown annually? As the <em>Hany</em> differs in different districts, it will be necessary to state this in <em>Morays</em> of <em>Mangalore</em>, or at least to state the proportion which the <em>Hany</em> of each district has to that measure.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>A.</em> 2,36,374 <em>Morays</em> of 60 <em>Mangalore Hanies</em>. N. B. This <em>Moray</em> contains 3,847½ cubical inches; the seed therefore is about 423,000 bushels.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Q.</em> 19th. What goods are exported by the sea from your portion of <em>Canara</em>, and to what annual amount?</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Q.</em> 20th. What goods are imported by sea, and to what annual amount?</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Q.</em> 21st. What goods are exported from your division of <em>Canara</em> by land, and to what aunual amount?</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-10"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="10" data-folio="4" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n9_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 4">[p. 4]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 15.</aside></div><p class="prose-p"><em>Q.</em> 22d. What goods are imported by land, and to what annual amount?</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>A.</em> Annexed are statements of the exports and imports by sea, from the revenue accompts, for two years during the government of the <em>Sultan</em>; and for one year, since the country has come under the government of the Company.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">General statement of commerce by sea.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">The particulars of this commerce will be seen by consulting these: I shall, however, state the general result.</p></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-001"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-001 · FOLIO 10</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/journey-from-madras-vol-iii/datasets/tables/tbl-001_p0010.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/journey-from-madras-vol-iii/datasets/tidy/tbl-001_p0010.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><caption>Account of the exports and imports into <em>Mangalore Taluc</em> (district) by sea.</caption><thead><tr><th scope="col" rowspan="2"></th><th scope="col" colspan="3">Imports.</th><th scope="col" colspan="3">Exports.</th></tr><tr><th scope="col"><em>Pagodas</em></th><th scope="col"><em>Fans.</em></th><th scope="col"><em>Anas.</em></th><th scope="col"><em>Pagodas</em></th><th scope="col"><em>Fans.</em></th><th scope="col"><em>Anas.</em></th></tr></thead><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td><em>Fusly</em> or revenue year 1203</td><td class="num">39,118</td><td class="num">5</td><td class="num">14¾</td><td class="num">58,581</td><td class="num">4</td><td class="num">2½</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Ditto - - - - - 1205</td><td class="num">13,641</td><td class="num">6</td><td class="num">2</td><td class="num">68,903</td><td class="num">0</td><td class="num">3</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Ditto - - - - - 1210</td><td class="num">84,461</td><td class="num">7</td><td class="num">19</td><td class="num">1,72,427</td><td class="num">2</td><td class="num">10</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">From this will be evident, the immense benefit that the country has received by a change of government.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Commerce by land.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">No custom-house accompt has been forwarded of the exports and imports by land; but Mr. Ravenshaw states the former to consist chiefly of salt, salt-fish, <em>Betel-nut</em>, ginger, coco-nuts, coco-nut oil, and raw-silk, to the annual amount of 20,388 <em>Pagodas</em>. The imports are chiefly cloths, cotton, thread, blankets, tobacco, and black cattle, with a small quantity of pepper, and sandal wood, to the amount of 37,455 <em>Pagodas</em>. The balance, in favour of the division of the province under Mr. Ravenshaw, is therefore 70,899 <em>Pagodas</em>, each worth at the mint price very nearly 8<em>s.</em> 0¾<em>d.</em></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Along with these answers to my queries, Mr. Ravenshaw most obligingly sent me some valuable statements relative to the quantity of seed required for rice lands, and to the quantity of produce,</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-11"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="11" data-folio="5" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n10_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 5">[p. 5]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><div RAVENSHAW. <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-19" data-page="19" data-folio="5" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n18_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 5">[p. 5]</button> of which I shall hereafter avail myself. He also favoured me with a statement of the population made up about this time; and reliance may be placed on its accuracy with respect to numbers. I have taken the liberty of altering the orthography, to make it conformable to the other parts of my account. The different casts are detailed in the usual confused manner, with which they are spoken of by the native officers of revenue.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Jan. 15.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. class="num">223</td><td class="num">73</td><td class="num">625</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">103</td><td><em>Lohar.</em> Blacksmiths</td><td class="num">127</td><td class="num">210</td><td class="num">101</td><td class="num">201</td><td class="num">95</td><td class="num">607</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">104</td><td><em>Julai.</em> Weavers</td><td class="num">847</td><td class="num">1367</td><td class="num">707</td><td class="num">1335</td><td class="num">543</td><td class="num">3952</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">105</td><td><em>Canara Kumbhara.</em> Pot-makers</td><td class="num">2188</td><td class="num">3892</td><td class="num">1570</td><td class="num">3646</td><td class="num">1350</td><td class="num">10458</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">106</td><td><em>Buddai.</em> Carpenters</td><td class="num">602</td><td class="num">986</td><td class="num">529</td><td class="num">4</td><td>—</td><td class="num">2</td><td>—</td><td class="num">6</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">108</td><td><em>Borudir.</em> Mat-makers</td><td class="num">65</td><td class="num">111</td><td class="num">55</td><td class="num">106</td><td class="num">39</td><td class="num">311</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">109</td><td><em>Tambutgars.</em> Coppersmiths</td><td class="num">5</td><td class="num">13</td><td class="num">9</td><td class="num">12</td><td class="num">5</td><td class="num">39</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">110</td><td><em>Chitrigar.</em> Painters</td><td class="num">5</td><td class="num">9</td><td class="num">5</td><td class="num">9</td><td class="num">4</td><td class="num">27</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">111</td><td><em>Pinjar.</em> Cotton-cleaners</td><td class="num">16</td><td class="num">27</td><td class="num">12</td><td class="num">28</td><td class="num">4</td><td class="num">71</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">112</td><td><em>Shiculdars.</em> Cutlers</td><td class="num">10</td><td class="num">26</td><td class="num">6</td><td class="num">23</td><td class="num">7</td><td class="num">62</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">113</td><td><em>Zeendar.</em> Saddlers</td><td class="num">32</td><td class="num">62</td><td class="num">26</td><td class="num">62</td><td class="num">25</td><td class="num">175</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">114</td><td><em>Dirzi.</em> Taylors</td><td class="num">125</td><td class="num">252</td><td class="num">119</td><td class="num">245</td><td class="num">87</td><td class="num">703</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">115</td><td><em>Toipha.</em> Dancers and musicians</td><td class="num">156</td><td class="num">140</td><td class="num">96</td><td class="num">345</td><td class="num">142</td><td class="num">723</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">116</td><td><em>Jetty.</em> Wrestlers</td><td class="num">2</td><td class="num">5</td><td class="num">3</td><td class="num">4</td><td class="num">1</td><td class="num">13</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-22" data-page="22" data-folio="8" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n21_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 8">[p. 8]</button> 117</td><td><em>Killabund.</em> Architects, literally constructors of forts</td><td class="num">4</td><td class="num">3</td><td>—</td><td class="num">7</td><td class="num">4</td><td class="num">14</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">118</td><td><em>Tapegar.</em> Jewellers</td><td class="num">1</td><td class="num">2</td><td class="num">2</td><td class="num">2</td><td class="num">1</td><td class="num">7</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">119</td><td><em>Jilligar.</em> People who search wells, and <em>tanks</em> for lost money</td><td class="num">5</td><td class="num">7</td><td class="num">5</td><td class="num">4</td><td class="num">2</td><td class="num">18</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">120</td><td><em>Moothaley.</em></td><td class="num">26</td><td class="num">35</td><td class="num">21</td><td class="num">27</td><td class="num">24</td><td class="num">107</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">121</td><td><em>Adagathur Mogayar.</em> Boatmen and fishermen</td><td class="num">31</td><td class="num">51</td><td class="num">18</td><td class="num">61</td><td class="num">14</td><td class="num">144</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td class="num">122</td><td><em>Corchey.</em> Day labourers</td><td class="num">3</td><td class="num">11</td><td class="num">4</td><td class="num">7</td><td class="num">11</td><td class="num">33</td></tr><tr class="row-total"><td></td><td>Grand total</td><td class="num">79856</td><td class="num">141681</td><td class="num">64952</td><td class="num">140302</td><td class="num">49737</td><td class="num">396672</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The general result is, that in the southern division of <em>Canara</em> there are 79,856 houses, inhabited by 396,672 persons; of whom</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Polygamy not owing to an excess of females.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">Males, Men - 141,681 Boys - 64,952 206,633 Females, Women 140,302 Girls - 49,737 190,039 This excess of males above the female population, which also has been found to prevail in the <em>Bara-mahal</em>, and other parts of the peninsula where an accurate census has been taken, entirely overthrows the doctrine upon which some ingenious reasoners have attempted to account for the prevalence of polygamy in warm climates.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Jan. 16. State of the country.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 16.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">16th <em>January</em>, 1801.—I went about two miles, said to be two cosses and a half, to a place called <em>Urigara</em>, or the <em>bank</em>. Immediately beyond <em>Cavar</em> I was ferried over a very wide inlet of the sea, which separates the province of <em>Malabar</em> from that of <em>Canara</em>; but the country called <em>Malayala</em> by the natives extends a considerable way farther north. My road all the way led along a narrow bank of sand, between the sea and the inlet. The surf, although larger than any that I have seen on this coast, is by no means so violent <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-23" data-page="23" data-folio="9" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n22_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 9">[p. 9]</button> as at Madras; and small fishing canoes go through it with ease. At <em>Urigara</em> the sand bank increases in width, and admits of some rice fields, and plantations of coco-nut trees. There is here no village; but there are a few huts inhabited by <em>Moplays</em>, who now possess the sea-coast of this part of <em>Malayala</em>, as the <em>Nairs</em> do the interior. On the side of the inlet, opposite from <em>Urigara</em>, is <em>Niléswara</em>, now a <em>Moplay</em> village, but formerly the residence of a <em>Rájá</em>, who derived his title from the place, which is called after one of the names of the god <em>Siva</em>. Although the <em>Nairs</em> are still more numerous than the <em>Moplays</em>, yet during <em>Tippoo’s</em> authority, while not protected by government, the <em>Hindus</em> were forced to skulk in the woods, and all such as could be caught were circumcised. It must be observed, that however involuntary this conversion may be, it is perfectly effectual, and the convert immediately becomes a good Mussulman, as otherwise he would have no cast at all; and, although the doctrine of cast be no part of the faith of Muhammed, it has in India been fully adopted by the low ranks of Mussulmans. On entering <em>Canara</em>, an immediate change in the police takes place. No person is here permitted to swagger about with arms: these may be kept in the house for protection against thieves; but they must not be brought into public, for the encouragement of assassination.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Jan. 17. Appearance of the country.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">17th <em>January.</em>—I went about ten miles to <em>Hosso-durga</em>, or <em>Pungal</em>-<em>cotay</em>; both of which signify the new fort, the former in the dialect of <em>Karnáta</em>, and the latter in the <em>Malayala</em> language. The country near the sea, most of the way that I came to-day, is low and sandy; but much of it is rice-land, intermixed with which is much sandy land, too poor, the natives say, to produce coco-nut palms. The whole appears to be much neglected, owing to a want of inhabitants.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. <em>Jan. 17.</em></aside></div><p class="prose-p">Towards <em>Hosso-durga</em>, the dry-field rises into gentle swells; yet it is too hard and dry for plantations. It is now waste; but, when there were plenty of people, it was cultivated for <em>Ragy</em> (<em>Cynosurus</em> <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-24" data-page="24" data-folio="10" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n23_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 10">[p. 10]</button> <em>corocanus</em>), Horse-gram (<em>Dolichos biflorus</em>), <em>Sesamum</em>, and different pulses. The hill-rice is here unknown; the soil, however, is exactly the same as that which is used to the southward for this grain.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote"><em>Hosso-durga.</em></aside></div><p class="prose-p">The fort is large, and well built of the <em>Laterite</em> common all over <em>Malayala</em>. The bastions being round, it is more capable of defence than the native forts in general, in which the defences are usually square. It occupies a fine rising ground, looks well at a distance, and commands a noble prospect. The only inhabitants are a few <em>Puttar Bráhmans</em>, who serve a temple, and whose ancestors were placed there by the <em>Ikeri Rájá</em>, who built the fort.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">History of the <em>Niléswara Rájás</em>.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 17.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. <em>Jan.</em> 17.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">According to the report of the <em>Nairs</em> here, all this part of the country originally belonged to <em>Colata-nada</em>; but from the river of <em>Cavai</em> to that near <em>Beäcul</em> had been long alienated, from the house of <em>Colastri</em>, to the <em>Niléswara Rájá</em>, a chief of the <em>Tamuri</em> family. In the year 905 (<em>A. D.</em> 17 <sup>29</sup>⁄<sub>30</sub>), <em>Ráma Varmá Rájá</em> of <em>Niléswara</em> was invaded by the <em>Ikeri Rájá</em>, who in the following year built the fort. After a struggle of twelve years, the <em>Nair</em> prince was compelled to become tributary. His country was divided into three <em>Nadas</em>, or districts, for each of which he agreed to pay annually 530 <em>Ikeri Pagodas</em>, or 213<em>l</em>. 12<em>s</em>. 3<em>d</em>. On paying this sum the <em>Rájás</em> were allowed to retain the entire management of their country, and seem at least so early to have established a regular land-tax in lieu of their claims on the moveable property of all persons dying in their territory. These claims they entirely relinquished, and took one half of the landlord’s (<em>Jenmcar’s</em>) profit on rice-lands, and one fifth of his profit on gardens. On the destruction of the <em>Ikeri</em> family, <em>Hyder</em> took possession of this country, and increased the tribute to 1500 <em>Pagodas</em> for each district; but allowed the <em>Rájá</em>, as collector, an establishment of 650 <em>Pagodas</em> a year; so that, in fact, each district paid 1283⅓ <em>Pagodas</em>, or 517<em>l</em>. 2<em>s</em>. 4½<em>d</em>. Some time afterwards, some landlords (<em>Jenmcars</em>) having made complaints of violent oppression against the <em>Rájá</em>, he resisted the people sent by <em>Hyder</em> to investigate the matter, and a war ensued, which ended in the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-25" data-page="25" data-folio="11" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n24_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 11">[p. 11]</button> <em>Rájá’s</em> being forced to an exile in <em>Travancore</em>. <em>Hyder</em> then took the country under his own management, and increased the rate of the land-tax; but, as usual, he made this more palatable by granting considerable allowances to the temples and <em>Bráhmans</em>. As soon as <em>Tippoo</em> obtained authority in the country, these were stopped; but, since the province was conquered by the Company, a part of the allowances have been given to the priests (<em>Pújáris</em>) who officiate in the temples. When General Mathews took <em>Bangalore</em>, the <em>Rájá</em> came back from <em>Travancore</em>, and seized on the country. After the <em>Sultan</em> had triumphantly made the peace of <em>Mangalore</em>, he was opposed with such success by this petty <em>Rájá</em>, that he was forced to consent that the <em>Rájá</em> should manage the country, and pay only the same tribute which had been exacted by <em>Hyder</em>. In the year 961 (<em>A. D.</em> 178 <sup>5</sup>⁄<sub>6</sub>), the <em>Rájá</em>, having been lulled into security, was inveigled, by repeated promises of safety and friendship, to visit <em>Budr’ uz Zamánkhán</em>, governor of <em>Beäcul</em>, who hanged him instantly, and, having marched all his forces into the country, before any measure could be taken to resist him, reduced the whole to the obedience of his master. The younger brother of <em>Ráma Varmá</em> made his escape to <em>Travancore</em>, and remained there until Lord Cornwallis invaded <em>Seringapatam</em>. He then came to <em>Tellichery</em>, from whence he received supplies of arms. In the year 966 (<em>A. D.</em> 179 <sup>0</sup>⁄<sub>1</sub>), he returned with these to <em>Niléswara</em>, raised an insurrection, and compelled the <em>Sultan</em> to allow him the management of the country, on condition of paying the former tribute. After the fall of <em>Seringapatam</em>, when Major Monro arrived to take charge of <em>Canara</em> as collector, the <em>Rájá</em> was sick, but sent his sister’s son, or heir, to wait on that gentleman; who very prudently told the <em>Rájá</em>, that his case would be laid before the government for their decision. In the mean while, the country was put entirely under the management of <em>Tahsildars</em>, exactly on the plan introduced by Colonel Read, under whom Major Monro had been instructed in civil affairs. The <em>Rájá</em> has thus been deprived of all power; and the favourable time <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-26" data-page="26" data-folio="12" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n25_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 12">[p. 12]</button> was chosen, when the terror inspired by the fall of <em>Seringapatam</em> rendered this easy to be done. The <em>Rájá</em> has been allowed, for his support, a remission of the land-tax on all his <em>Cherical</em> lands, or private estate. The <em>Nairs</em>, however, complain of a want of good faith in the British officers. They allege, that General Hartley, on his return from <em>Seringapatam</em>, promised the <em>Rájá</em> that he should be continued in the management of the country.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The dominions of the <em>Niléswara Rájá</em> extended from the sea to the <em>Ghats</em>; and, according to the report of the same <em>Nairs</em>, are exceedingly depopulated by war, and by a famine that ensued while they were forced to retire into the woods to avoid circumcision. The inner parts of the country are much overgrown with woods, and are very thinly inhabited. Like the other parts of <em>Malayala</em>, they consist of alternate low hills and narrow vallies. In cultivation, more slaves than free men are employed.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote"><em>Jan.</em> 18. Appearance of the country.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">18th <em>January.</em>—I went an easy stage to <em>Beäcul</em>. From <em>Pungal</em>-<em>cotay</em>, to a river bounding the country of the <em>Niléswara Rájá</em> to the north, the road leads along a ridge, sloping very gently towards the sea, and rather steeper towards a narrow valley now covered with the second crop of rice. Beyond this are low hills. The soil of the ridge is extremely sandy, and the country is very bare. The river is not wide, and has at its mouth some low land well planted with coco-nut trees.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Between the river and <em>Beäcul</em> the low hills come close down to the sea side, and are very little intermixed with rice land. In the whole way I crossed only one narrow field. The hills, however, are not steep, and seem all to be capable of being laboured by the plough; but no traces of cultivation are visible.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote"><em>Beäcul.</em></aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 18.</aside></div><p class="prose-p"><em>Beäcul</em> is a strong native fort, placed, like <em>Cananore</em>, on a high point projecting into the sea towards the south, and having within it a bay. The town stands north from the fort, and contains forty or fifty houses scattered about in great confusion. The inhabitants are chiefly <em>Moplays</em> and <em>Mucuas</em>, with a few <em>Tiars</em>, and people of <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-27" data-page="27" data-folio="13" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n26_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 13">[p. 13]</button> <em>Kankána</em>, who have been long settled in <em>Canara</em> as shop-keepers. The country extending between the river south from <em>Beäcul</em>, and that near <em>Chandra-giri</em>, was divided into two districts (<em>Nadas</em>), which continued subject to the <em>Cherical Rájás</em>, as representatives of the house of <em>Colastri</em>, until the invasion by the <em>Ikeri Rájá</em>. Beggars begin to swarm here, as is the case almost every where in India in which I have been, except <em>Malabar</em>, where I scarcely met with one.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Produce of the rice-grounds.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">The <em>Tahsildar</em> (collector) says, that in the part of <em>Malayala</em> which is contained in <em>Canara</em>, the rice-lands near the sea produce annually only one crop, and yield from 5 to 10 seeds, or from 12½ to 25 bushels an acre. In the vallies of the inland country the produce is greater; the land that produces one crop only gives from 12 to 15 seeds, or from 24 to 37½ bushels an acre; that which gives two crops, produces the same quantity in the first, and from 8 to 10 seeds in the second, or from 20 to 25 bushels an acre. More grain is raised in the country than the small number of inhabitants can consume. The people are accused by the <em>Tahsildar</em> of excessive indolence, and of drunkenness; vices which he attributes to the constant troubles that prevailed during the government of the <em>Sultan</em>.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Revenue.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote"><em>Jan.</em> 18.</aside></div><p class="prose-p"><em>Trimula Row</em>, the <em>Tahsildar</em>, says, that the nominal value of this part of <em>Malayala</em> which is contained in <em>Canara</em>, according to the revenue accompts of <em>Tippoo’s</em> officers, was 8000 <em>Bahádary Varáhas</em>, or 32,000 <em>Rupees</em>. Although Major Monro did not make any formal remission of this rent, he only levied 6000 <em>Pagodas</em>, or 24,000 <em>Rupees</em>, and did not keep the remainder as a balance against the cultivators, which would have depressed their spirits. He took from each man, what in his present circumstances he could afford to pay, and did not, for the sake of a nominal revenue on paper, prevent all exertion in the cultivator, by holding over his head the terror of a balance which he could never hope to clear. The rice ground now is not taxed by any share of the <em>Varum</em>, or neat rent; but each field pays so much, according to its supposed value; and this tax <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-28" data-page="28" data-folio="14" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n27_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 14">[p. 14]</button> is alleged to consume the whole rent. Very few of the landlords (<em>Jenmcars</em>) remain, and even the mortgagees (<em>Canumcars</em>) are willing to give up all the land, which they cannot cultivate with their own stock, to any one who will pay the land-tax. The gardens here pay not only a tax on the trees, as in <em>Malabar</em>, but also a tax on the extent of ground which they occupy; yet by <em>Trimula Row</em> they are reckoned by far the most profitable heritage for the cultivators. He thinks that the taxes on the cultivator are heavier here than those in <em>Arcot</em>. I must observe, that with all these complaints there is little of the rice-land waste; while there is no tax on the cultivation of dry grains, and very little of them is sown.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote"><em>Niléswara Rájá.</em></aside></div><p class="prose-p"><em>Trimula Row</em> says, that <em>Poduga</em> and <em>Cavi</em>, the two districts formerly belonging to <em>Cherical</em>, had been entirely subdued; but that the <em>Niléswara Rájás</em> had constantly disputed the authority of <em>Tippoo</em>. They frequently were able to retain the management, on condition of paying tribute, and then again were frequently driven into exile. The <em>Rájá</em> asked nothing more, from Major Monro, than a remission of the taxes on the <em>Cherical</em> lands, which was last year granted; but it is uncertain whether or not this favour will be continued.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote"><em>Jan.</em> 19. <em>Appearance of the country.</em></aside></div><p class="prose-p">19th <em>January.</em>—I went to a temple dedicated to <em>Iswara</em>, at a place called <em>Pulla</em>. The first part of my journey was over a sandy spit, separating a salt water lake from the sea. Beyond this, the country rises into open rising lands, all the way to <em>Chandra-giri</em> river, which is the northern boundary of <em>Malayala</em>. This rising land is in very few places too steep for the plough, and these places are in general rocky. The whole of this land is totally waste, and looks very ill, being covered with long withered grass. There are traces of its having been formerly cultivated; and, no doubt, with manure it would be productive of dry grains. For the cultivation of rice, tanks or reservoirs might easily be constructed; but, with the present paucity of inhabitants, it would be madness to cultivate any thing, except the richest spots. Intermixed with this rising land are a few plots of rice-ground, surrounded by palm gardens <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-29" data-page="29" data-folio="15" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n28_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 15">[p. 15]</button> and the houses of the <em>Nairs</em>; but the proportion of this rich land does not seem to be above a hundredth part of the country.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Jan. 19. <em>Chandra-giri.</em></aside></div><p class="prose-p"><em>Chandra-giri</em> is a large square fort, situated high above the river on its southern bank. It was built, like the other forts beforementioned, by <em>Sivuppa Náyaka</em>, the first prince of the house of <em>Ikeri</em> that established his authority in this part of <em>Canara</em>.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">South boundary of <em>Tulava</em>.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">At low water the river is shallow, but very wide. The country on its north side is by the <em>Hindus</em> called <em>Tulava</em>, and resembles that through which I passed on the south side of the river. I left to my right another fort named <em>Casselgoda</em>, which also was built by <em>Sivuppa</em>, when he subjected the petty <em>Rájás</em> of <em>Tulava</em>. <em>Pulla</em>, where I stopped, is on the banks of a salt water lake, communicating both with the sea and with the <em>Chandra-giri</em> river.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Jan. 20. Face of the country.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">20th January.—I went about ten miles to <em>Kanya-pura</em>, and about half way crossed a river of considerable width; yet at low water it is shallow. The country through which I passed resembles much the part of <em>Tulava</em> that I saw yesterday, but the plantations of coco-nuts were rather more numerous. The rice grounds are more neatly cultivated than those in <em>Malayala</em>, and the water for the second crop is conducted to them with great care. In many places, where the ground is too high to give a second crop of rice, a crop of <em>Ricinus</em>, or of sweet potatoes (<em>Convolvulus</em>), is taken. Near the sea, sugar-cane is cultivated. Many traces of former gardens are to be seen from the road, which shows that this kind of cultivation may be greatly extended.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote"><em>Kanya-pura</em>, and <em>Cumly</em>.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 20.</aside></div><p class="prose-p"><em>Kanya-pura</em> is seated on the south bank of a river which surrounds the fort and town of <em>Cumly</em>. This is situated on a high peninsula in a salt water lake, which is separated from the sea by a spit of sand. Two rivers fall into this kind of lake, and contain between them the peninsula on which <em>Cumly</em> stands. By far the greater part of the coast is occupied by a chain of salt water lakes; but the necks of land interposed render them of little use for an inland navigation. <em>Kanya-pura</em> contains about 200 houses, and <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-30" data-page="30" data-folio="16" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n29_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 16">[p. 16]</button> <em>Cumly</em> about 150. The inhabitants are chiefly <em>Moplays</em>, <em>Mucuas</em>, <em>Mogayers</em>, and <em>Kankanies</em>. The interior parts are chiefly occupied by the <em>Bráhmans</em> of <em>Tulava</em>, and the <em>Bunts</em>, or <em>Buntar</em>.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote"><em>Bráhmans of Tulava.</em></aside></div><p class="prose-p">The <em>Tulava Bráhmans</em> resemble the <em>Namburis</em>, and consider themselves as the proper lords of the country.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote"><em>Massadi Bunts.</em></aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 20.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">The <em>Buntar</em> are the highest rank of <em>Súdras</em> in <em>Tulava</em>, and resemble the <em>Nairs</em> of <em>Malayala</em>. Having assembled some reputable persons of this cast, they gave me the following account of their customs. They are of three kinds: <em>Massadi Bunts</em>, or <em>Buntar</em> properly so called; <em>Jain</em>; and <em>Parivarada Buntar</em>. The <em>Massadi Bunts</em> are those whom I here examined. They can eat and drink with the <em>Nairs</em>; but the two casts have no sexual intercourse. They do not pretend to be by birth soldiers; their proper duty is the cultivation of the land. They can keep accompts, but are not admitted to any higher kind of learning. They have head-men, called <em>Mocustas</em>, one for every district. The office is hereditary in the males by the female line; the same mode of succession prevailing here, as in <em>Malayala</em>. At present, this office merely confers dignity; the officers of government having assumed all the jurisdiction that formerly belonged to the <em>Mocustas</em>, who settled disputes not only relative to casts, but also concerning property. In general, all the brothers and unmarried sisters of a family live together in the same house. All the property belonging to the family is considered as common, and is managed, for the good of the whole, by the oldest male. A man’s own children are not his heirs. During his life-time he may give them money; but all of which he dies possessed goes to his sisters, and to their children. If a man has a mother’s-brother’s-daughter, he must marry her; but he may take two or three wives beside. The ceremony is performed by the girl’s father, or other near kinsman. When a man marries several wives, none of them can leave him without his consent; but when discord runs high, he in general sends one of the disputants back to her brother’s house; and then she is at liberty to marry again. A man at any time, if he <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-31" data-page="31" data-folio="17" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n30_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 17">[p. 17]</button> dislikes his wife, may send her back to her brother’s house; and he can do no more if she has committed adultery. In all these cases, or when a widow returns to her brother’s house on her husband’s death, she is accompanied by her children, and may marry again, unless she has committed adultery with a person of low cast; but if that crime has been committed with a <em>Bráhman</em>, <em>Kshatri</em>, <em>Vaisya</em>, or <em>Bunt</em>, she is well received, her children become her brother’s heirs, and no man will have any objection to marry her. The <em>Buntar</em> are permitted to eat animal food, and to drink spirituous liquors. They burn the dead. They seem to be entirely ignorant of a state of future existence; only they believe, that such men as die accidental deaths become <em>Pysáchi</em>, or evil spirits, and are exceedingly troublesome, by making extraordinary noises in families, and occasioning fits, and other diseases, especially in women. To expel these, the <em>Buntar</em> apply to the <em>Nucaru</em>, who are a class similar to the <em>Cunian</em> of <em>Malayala</em>, and who pretend by means of incantations (<em>Mantrams</em>) to have a power over the spirits. For the same purpose, sacrifices are offered to various <em>Saktis</em>, which differ in almost every different village. Those worshipped here are <em>Dumawutty</em>, <em>Iberabuta</em>, or the twin devils, and <em>Birnala</em>. Besides the sacrifices offered to these idols, to free the people from the attacks of the <em>Pysáchi</em>, <em>Iberabuta</em> and <em>Birnala</em> must be appeased by an annual, and <em>Dumawutty</em> by a monthly sacrifice. If these are omitted, the enraged devils kill both man and beast. <em>Siva</em>, however, is the proper deity of the cast; yet the <em>Buntar</em> pray also to <em>Vishnu</em>. They call the <em>Tulava Bráhmans</em> their <em>Puróhitas</em>; but on no occasion do these read <em>Mantrams</em> for their followers. All that they can do is to receive <em>Dharma</em>, or charity, and to bestow consecrated ashes and holy water.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote"><em>Cumly Rájá</em>.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan, 20.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Invasion by the <em>Coorg Rájá</em>.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">State of the natives in <em>Tulava</em>.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">All this south part of <em>Tulava</em> formerly belonged to the <em>Cumly Rájá</em>, who pretends to be a <em>Kshatri</em> from the north of India. The manners of his family are the same with those of the <em>Rájás</em> of <em>Malayala</em>. All the males keep <em>Nair</em> girls; but their children, who are <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-32" data-page="32" data-folio="18" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n31_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 18">[p. 18]</button> called <em>Tambans</em>, have no right to the succession. The eldest daughter in the female line cohabits with a <em>Tulava Bráhman</em>; her sons become <em>Rájás</em>, and her eldest daughter continues the line of the family. Whenever she pleases, she changes her <em>Bráhman</em>. The younger daughters also cohabit with <em>Bráhmans</em>, and produce a race of people called <em>Bayllal</em>, who have no right to the succession. The dominions of this family extended from the <em>Chandra-giri</em> river to that on the north side of <em>Cumly</em>, and produced an annual revenue of 15,000 <em>Ikeri Pagodas</em>, or 6044<em>l</em>. 3<em>s</em>. 4<em>d</em>. The <em>Rájá</em> lives now in the country; but he has neither lands nor authority. Before the last war he lived at <em>Tellichery</em>, on a pension from the Company; which has been doubled since we got possession of the country of his ancestors.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The interior parts are said to be naturally very fertile in rice, but they suffered much in the last war. The <em>Coorg Rájá</em>, during the siege of <em>Seringapatam</em>, under pretence of assisting the English, made an incursion into the country, and swept away all the inhabitants that he could seize. He has given them possessions in his own country; but they are very desirous of returning home, although I do not hear that he uses them ill.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The people of <em>Tulava</em>, although longer subjected to a foreign yoke than those of <em>Malabar</em>, never have been so entirely subdued as the greater part of the <em>Hindus</em>, and have always been able successfully to resist the pretensions of their governors to be proprietors of the soil. Their native chiefs have, indeed, been in general able to retain more or less of the management of the country; and on the fall of <em>Seringapatam</em>, I am here informed, were very much disposed to try how far they could assert their independence. Two months are said to have elapsed, after the arrival of Major Monro in the country, before that gentleman could induce the people to meet him for the purpose of settling the revenue; but the decisive measures adopted to punish all those who presumed to disturb the peace, an assumed severity of manner to prevent the hopes of success from cajolery, and a strict forbearance from <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-33" data-page="33" data-folio="19" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n32_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 19">[p. 19]</button> making promises or concessions for the sake of a temporary submission, have saved <em>Canara</em> from anarchy, and destructive, though petty warfare.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Jan. 21. Appearance of the country.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">21st <em>January</em>.—I ferried over the lake to the peninsula on which <em>Cumly</em> stands, and which was formerly joined to <em>Kanya-pura</em> by a bridge. The situation of the fort is very fine, and the town has formerly been pretty considerable. The two rivers leave a narrow isthmus of rice-fields. At present, both the rivers and the lake are salt; but in the rainy season they are quite fresh, and at that time, when no boats can venture to sea, might afford a fine supply of fish: this, however, is an article of food which, except by persons of very low cast, is seldom used. Having crossed the north branch, I went along the sea-beach, having on my right high sandy downs, which prevented me from seeing the country, until I arrived at the banks of a wide but fordable river. On the north side of this is a large straggling town called <em>Manjéswara</em>. It contains many good houses, chiefly inhabited by <em>Moplays</em>, <em>Buntar</em>, and <em>Biluars</em>. Having crossed the plain on which <em>Manjéswara</em> stands, and forded a small river, I took up my quarters at a town named <em>Hosso-betta</em>, or the <em>new-strength</em>, which is situated on a steep bank that overhangs the last mentioned river.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote"><em>Byrasu Wodears</em>, a <em>Jain</em> family.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Petty <em>Rájás</em> of <em>Tulava</em>.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">Immediately after crossing the northern branch of the <em>Cumly</em> river, you enter a country that formerly belonged to a <em>Jain</em> family called <em>Byrasu Wodear</em>, which resided at <em>Carculla</em>. The <em>Jain</em> here say, that this family were overthrown by <em>Sivuppa Náyaka</em> of <em>Ikeri</em>, who divided the country into small districts, each producing an annual revenue of from one to three thousand <em>Pagodas</em>. Over each of these was placed a petty <em>Rájá</em> of the <em>Jain</em> religion. Ever since, the country has been constantly on the decline, having been continually in a state of insurrection or confusion.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote"><em>Bungar Rájá</em>.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">The dominions of the first of these <em>Jain</em> chiefs that I entered were those of the <em>Bungar Rájá</em>. <em>Tippoo</em> hanged the last person who <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-34" data-page="34" data-folio="20" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n33_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 20">[p. 20]</button> possessed this dignity; and his children cultivate some land at <em>Nandavara</em>, a village in the territory of the family.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Jan. 21. <em>Rájá</em> of <em>Vitly.</em></aside></div><p class="prose-p"><em>Hosso-betta</em> is also frequently called <em>Vitly Manjéswara</em>, from its having belonged to another <em>Jain</em> chief named <em>Hegady Rájá</em> of <em>Vitly</em>. By the intervention of other districts it is however entirely separated from the other territory which belonged to the <em>Vitly Rájás</em>, the last of whom was hanged here about three months ago. Before the war, he had lived at <em>Tellichery</em>, and received from the Company a monthly pension of 200 <em>Rupees</em>. When the army of General Harris approached <em>Seringapatam</em>, the <em>Rájá</em> came here, and, having collected a rabble, plundered the country with great success, and then returned to <em>Tellichery</em>. After <em>Canara</em> became subject to the Company, the people, who had been thus wantonly plundered, applied for redress, and <em>Hegady</em> was required to restore their property. This he refused, and, having procured 800 muskets, it is said from <em>Mousa</em>, he returned to <em>Vitly</em>, dressed up some ruffians like <em>Sepoys</em>, and assumed the authority of a sovereign prince. For almost a year he was able to skulk about the woods, and support himself by plunder; but having been then taken, he was immediately hanged, ever since which the country has been perfectly quiet.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote"><em>Kankánies</em> expelled from <em>Goa.</em></aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 21.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">The principal inhabitants of <em>Hosso-betta</em>, and indeed of many of the towns in <em>Tulava</em>, are <em>Kankánies</em>, or people descended from natives of <em>Kankána</em>. They say, that they fled hither, to avoid a persecution at <em>Govay (Goa)</em>, their native country. An order arrived from the king of Portugal to convert all the natives. The viceroy, when this order arrived, was, they say, a very lenient good man, and permitted all the natives who chose to retire to carry their effects with them, and allowed them fifteen days to arrange their affairs. Accordingly, all the rich people, <em>Bráhmans</em> and <em>Súdras</em>, retired to <em>Tulava</em>, with such of their property as they could in that time realise, and they now chiefly subsist by trade. Both <em>Bráhmans</em> and <em>Súdras</em> are called by the national appellation of <em>Kankánies</em>, and the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-35" data-page="35" data-folio="21" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n34_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 21">[p. 21]</button> other <em>Bráhmans</em> will have no communion with these exiles. They are, however, in flourishing circumstances; and I saw some of their marriage processions passing to-day, attended by a number of exceedingly well dressed people, and very handsome girls. The poor <em>Kankánies</em> who remained behind at <em>Goa</em> were, of course, all converted to what was called Christianity.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Jan. 22. <em>Ulala.</em></aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote"><em>Harawurry Manjéswara.</em></aside></div><p class="prose-p">22d <em>January.</em>—I went a short stage to <em>Ulala</em>, a large town on the south side of the lake of <em>Mangalore</em>, and formerly the residence of a petty prince. I first passed through <em>Harawurry Manjéswara</em>, which is immediately north from the <em>Manjéswara</em> that belonged to the <em>Vitly Rájá</em>; but it is situated in the district surrounding <em>Mangalore</em>, which was not divided among the petty <em>Rájás</em>, but was immediately under the government of the lieutenant of the <em>Ikeri Rájá</em> who commanded at <em>Mangalore</em>.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Harbour of <em>Mangalore.</em></aside></div><p class="prose-p">I afterwards crossed over the lake to the town, where I remained until the 29th. The lake is a fine body of salt water, separated from the sea by a beach of sand. In this, formerly, there was one opening; the depth of water in which was such, that ships of a considerable burthen, after their cargo had been removed, could enter the lake. Last year a new opening formed in the beach, which has proved very injurious to the harbour. The depth of the old opening has diminished, and that of the new one has never become great; so that now, even at high water, and in easy weather, vessels drawing more than ten feet cannot enter.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote"><em>Mangalore.</em></aside></div><p class="prose-p">For a native place of strength, the fort of <em>Mangalore</em> was well constructed; but was destroyed by <em>Tippoo</em>, after he had found how little his fortresses were calculated to resist European soldiers, and with what difficulty he could retake any of them, that were garrisoned by a few British troops. The town, called also <em>Codeal Bundar</em>, is large, and is built round the sides of the peninsula, in the elevated center of which the fort was placed. The lake, by which the peninsula is formed, is a most beautiful piece of salt water. The <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-36" data-page="36" data-folio="22" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n35_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 22">[p. 22]</button> boats that ply on it are execrable; and the fishermen by whom they are managed are a very indolent drunken race.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Jan. 22. Customs of <em>the Mogayer.</em></aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 22.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">These fishermen are called <em>Mogayer</em>, and are a cast of <em>Tulava</em> origin. They resemble the <em>Mucuas</em> of <em>Malayala</em>, but the one cast will have no communion with the other. The <em>Mogayer</em> are boatmen, fishermen, porters, and palanquin bearers. All of this cast can eat and intermarry together. They pretend to be <em>Súdras</em> of a pure descent, which is rather doubtful; and assume a superiority over the <em>Halepecas</em>, one of the most common casts of cultivators in <em>Tulava</em>; but they acknowledge themselves greatly inferior to the <em>Bunts</em>. They have head-men called <em>Gurucaras</em>, whose office is hereditary in the males by the female line. With the assistance of a council, the head-man settles disputes, and punishes all transgressions against the rules of cast. The only fault that is punishable with excommunication is when a woman commits fornication with a person of a lower cast; but for adultery with either a man of the cast, or of one that is higher, a woman is seldom turned away by her husband; and even if she be, she is by no means disgraced, but returns to her brother’s house, and may be married again whenever she finds a new lover. The men may take several wives, and the whole ceremony of marriage consists in giving the girl some ornaments. After accepting these, she must live in his house, nor can she leave it without her husband’s consent; but, whenever he pleases, he may send her back to her brother. The children always follow the mother, and are the heirs to her brothers, and not to their father. If a man’s sister be living in the house, she has the entire management of it, and his wives have no authority. The <em>Mogayer</em> are permitted to eat animal food, and to drink intoxicating liquors. Some few of them can read, and write accompts. Those of them who are rich burn, those who are poor bury their dead. The spirits of good men go to <em>Moesha</em>, which, according to the <em>Bráhmans</em>, is the heaven where <em>Vishnu</em> resides; but the <em>Mogayer</em> <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-37" data-page="37" data-folio="23" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n36_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 23">[p. 23]</button> know of no other. After death, bad men are supposed to be taken by <em>Emma Dharma Raja</em>, the judge of the infernal regions. Some of the <em>Mogayers</em> pray to <em>Vishnu</em>, and some to <em>Siva</em>; but the proper deity of the cast is a goddess named <em>Restali Mahastumma</em>, who is represented by an image in the form of a woman. The priest (<em>Pújari</em>) is a <em>Biluar</em>, whose office is hereditary in the males of the female line. The women of this family live with laymen, and the daughters of these are kept by the priest. This is the only kind of priest that these people have. The <em>Brahmans</em> indeed accept <em>Dharma</em> (duty) from them; but they do not attend at any of their ceremonies, to read <em>Mantrams</em>. The goddess has other worshippers, <em>Buntar</em>, and oil-makers. She never occasions any trouble to her votaries, if they pray and offer sacrifices; but, if these are neglected, she inflicts sickness on the impious persons. Men who have incurred her displeasure, and who in consequence have become sick, make a vow to suspend themselves by hooks passed through the skin of their backs, and thus to be swung round before her temple. This expiation is performed at the <em>Játram</em>, or great annual feast, when many bloody sacrifices are offered. Women who suppose that the goddess has inflicted on them barrenness, or other great infirmity, vow to walk barefooted on red-hot coals before the temple. If the goddess hears their prayers, she prevents the coals from burning their feet. My informants impudently assert, that the ceremony is frequently performed. A quantity of red-hot coals are spread before the temple; and the woman, after having fasted a whole day, walks three times slowly with bare feet over the fire. The <em>Mogayers</em> suppose themselves liable to various diseases from the influence of evil spirits, called <em>Jacny</em>, and <em>Teiteno</em>, which resemble those called <em>Paisáchi</em>. These are not to be expelled by sacrifices; but the <em>Mogayer</em> apply to some <em>Biluaras</em>, and Mussulmans, who possess invocations (<em>Mantrams</em>) fit for the purpose.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Christians of <em>Kankána</em> settled in <em>Tulava</em>.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. <em>Jan. 22.</em></aside></div><p class="prose-p">The princes of the house of <em>Ikeri</em> had given great encouragement to the Christians, and had induced 80,000 of them to settle in <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-38" data-page="38" data-folio="24" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n37_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 24">[p. 24]</button> <em>Tulava.</em> They are all of <em>Kankána</em> descent, and retained the language, dress, and manners of the people of that country. The clergy, it is true, adopted the dress of the order to which they belonged; but they are all natives descended from <em>Kankána</em> families, and were purposely educated in a seminary at <em>Goa</em>, where they were instructed in the Portuguese and Latin languages, and in the doctrines of the Church of Rome. In <em>Tulava</em> they had 27 churches, each provided with a vicar, and the whole under the control of a vicar-general, subject to the authority of the archbishop of <em>Goa</em>. <em>Tippoo</em> threw the priests into dungeons, forcibly converted to <em>Islámism</em> the laity, and destroyed all the churches. As the Christian religion does not prevent the readmission into the church of such delinquents, these involuntary Mussulmans have in general reconciled themselves with the clergy, who now of course are at liberty, and 15,000 have already returned to <em>Mangalore</em> and its vicinity; 10,000 made their escape to <em>Malabar</em>, from whence they are returning home as quickly as their poverty will admit. The clergy are now busy with their flocks, whose poverty, however, has hitherto prevented them from rebuilding any of their churches. During the government of <em>Hyder</em>, these Christians were possessed of considerable estates in land, all of which were confiscated by <em>Tippoo</em>, and immediately bestowed on persons of other casts, from whom it would be difficult to resume them. These poor people have none of the vices usually attributed to the native Portuguese; and their superior industry is more readily acknowledged by the neighbouring <em>Hindus</em>, than avowed by themselves. The vicar-general was long confined in <em>Jamál-ábád</em>. He speaks Latin neither correctly nor with fluency, and seems very desirous of obtaining what he calls a domineering power over the sect, that his authority may be equal to that of the native <em>Gurus</em>; so as to keep his flock in good order, not only by the spiritual means of excommunication, but also by the temporal expedients of fine and corporal punishment.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-39"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="39" data-folio="25" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n38_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 25">[p. 25]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 22. Coin.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">The coins in common currency here are,</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><em>Gold.</em></h3><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-007"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-007 · FOLIO 39</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/journey-from-madras-vol-iii/datasets/tables/tbl-007_p0039.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/journey-from-madras-vol-iii/datasets/tidy/tbl-007_p0039.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><caption><em>Gold.</em></caption><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td>The <em>Ikeri Varaha</em>, or <em>Pagoda</em> struck by the princes of <em>Ikeri</em>, exchanges for</td><td><em>Rupees</em> 4</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>The <em>Bahadary Varaha</em>, or <em>Pagoda</em> struck by <em>Hyder</em></td><td class="num">4</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>The <em>Sultany</em> ditto, <em>Pagoda</em> coined by <em>Tippoo</em></td><td class="num">4</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>The <em>Krishna Raja</em> ditto, <em>Pagoda</em> coined by the present <em>Mysore Raja</em></td><td class="num">4</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>The <em>Puli Varaha</em>, star <em>Pagoda</em> of <em>Madras</em></td><td class="num">3½</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>The <em>Feringy Petta Varaha</em>, or <em>Porto-novo Pagoda</em></td><td class="num">3</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>The <em>Sultany</em>, <em>Canter’-Raya</em>, or <em>Ikeri Hunas</em> or <em>Fanams</em></td><td>⅕</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>The <em>Vir’-Raya Huna</em>, or <em>Fanam</em> coined by the <em>Coorg Raja</em></td><td>¼</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><em>Silver.</em></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Suráti Rupiya</em>, the <em>Rupee</em> coined at <em>Surat</em>, worth silver <em>Fanams</em> 5½ <em>Company Rupiya</em>, the <em>Madras Rupee</em> lately introduced, ditto 5½ <em>Bily Huna</em>, the same silver <em>Fanam</em> that is current in <em>Malabar</em>. In the <em>Bázár</em> it exchanges for 10 <em>Dudus</em>, or <em>Dubs</em>, but in revenue is taken for 14.</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><em>Copper.</em></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Both the <em>Any Dudus</em>, or <em>Tippoo’s</em> copper <em>Dubs</em>, and the <em>Bombay Paisa</em>, coined in England, are current here; and these with their fractions, ⅓, ½, and ¼, are the only small coin in use. <em>Cowries</em>, or small shells, are not in circulation.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In payment for goods, or debts, every person must receive these coins at the above rate of exchange. The money-changers give silver for gold at the regulated price; but they take a small <em>Batta</em>, or exchange, when they give gold for silver. They give copper for silver at the regulated price; but demand 10½ <em>Dubs</em> for the silver <em>Fanam</em>.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Accompts.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 22.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">Merchants accompts are commonly kept in <em>Sultany Pagodas</em>, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-40" data-page="40" data-folio="26" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n39_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 26">[p. 26]</button> <em>Rupees</em>, and <em>Anas</em>, or fractions of 16 parts; others are kept in <em>Pagodas</em>, a nominal <em>Huna</em> of 10 to the <em>Pagoda</em>, and <em>Anas</em>, or 16 parts of these <em>Hunas</em>.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">I shall make my alculations by reducing all sums to <em>Sultany Pagodas</em>, and taking these at their mint value of a little more than 8<em>s</em>.</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><em>Weights.</em></h3><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Weights.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">The <em>Seer</em> (<em>Sida</em>) used for weighing ought to equal 24 <em>Bombay Rupees</em>, those in common currency having from 178 to 179 grains. I weighed a <em>Seer</em> in common use in the market (<em>Bazar</em>), and found, that it contained 4297 grains, which is more than the standard of 24 <em>Rupees</em>. The <em>Seer</em> is divided into halves, quarters, eighths, and sixteenths.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The <em>Maund</em> (<em>Mana</em>) by which goods are sold in the market, contains 46 <em>Seers</em>, or 28 <sup>14</sup>/<sub>100</sub> lb.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The <em>Maund</em> by which the merchants purchase weighs 16 <em>Rupees</em> more, or is 28 <sup>55</sup>/<sub>100</sub> lb. This is the weight by which the Company buys and sells.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Jagory</em> is both bought and sold by a <em>Maund</em> of 40 <em>Seers</em>, or 24 <sup>47</sup>/<sub>100</sub> lb.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The <em>Candy</em> (<em>Baru</em>) contains 20 <em>Maunds</em>, and varies, accordingly, from 571 lb. to 489 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>2</sub> lb. These calculations are founded on the weight of the <em>Rupee</em>. If the <em>Seer</em> that I weighed were taken as a standard, we must to the above mentioned weights add about one-third per cent.</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><em>Grain Measures.</em></h3><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Dry-measure.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">These differ not only in every village, but also as they are used for retailing grain in the market, for purchasing grain from the farmer, or for sowing the seed. These differences have, no doubt, been introduced in order to confuse the officers of revenue.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 22.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">For retailing in the market here, the <em>Seer</em> (<em>Sida</em>) is formed by mixing equal quantities of salt and of the nine most common grains; <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-41" data-page="41" data-folio="27" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n40_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 27">[p. 27]</button> and then, by taking of the mixture 84 <em>Bombay Rupees</em> weight. This quantity, when heaped, fills a <em>Seer</em> measure, and is 73 <sup>683</sup>⁄<sub>1000</sub> cubical inches. The <em>Moray</em>, or <em>Mudi</em>, contains 38 <em>Seers</em>, or about 1 <sup>3</sup>⁄<sub>10</sub> bushel.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The grain measure by which the farmers sell their crops is thus formed:</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">64 <sup>125</sup>⁄<sub>1000</sub> cubical inches = 1 <em>Hany</em>. 14 <em>Hanies</em> - - = 1 <em>Cullishigay</em>. 3 <em>Cullishigays</em> - = 1 <em>Mudi</em> or <em>Moray</em>, or 1 <sup>2525</sup>⁄<sub>10000</sub> bushel.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Grain, salt, and sometimes pepper, are sold by measure. Of this last a <em>Pucka Seer</em>, or 73 <sup>683</sup>⁄<sub>1000</sub> cubical inches, is reckoned to weigh 51 <sup>1</sup>⁄<sub>2</sub> <em>Bombay Rupees</em>.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Calendar.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">In <em>Tulava</em> the era of <em>Sáliváhanam</em> is in use, and at <em>Mangalore</em> this is reckoned the year 1722; but in the north it is reckoned the year 1723, and the people there are certainly the most learned. The year of <em>Tulava</em> is solar. I here give an almanack for the current year, according to the <em>Bráhmans</em> of <em>Carculla</em>, who agree with those above the <em>Ghats</em> concerning the time of the era.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. <em>Jan. 22.</em></aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 22.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. <em>Jan.</em> 22.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. class="row-data"><td></td><td class="num">9</td><td class="num">19</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr></tbody></table></div></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Pretensions of the <em>Tulava Bráhmans</em>.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">The <em>Bráhmans</em> of <em>Tulava</em>, like the <em>Namburis</em>, pretend, that the country was created expressly for their use by <em>Parasu-ráma</em>, and that they are the only persons entitled to be called <em>Baliky</em>, or proprietors of the soil. It would not appear, however, that in <em>Tulava</em> this story was ever so successful as it has been in <em>Malayala</em>. The <em>Bráhmans</em> indeed say, that they did not like the country, and were always running away to a city named <em>Ahichaytra</em>, which seems to be in <em>Telingána</em>. At length a prince, named <em>Myuru Varmá</em>, made all those here adopt some new customs; after which the <em>Panch</em>-<em>Drávida Bráhmans</em> of <em>Ahichaytra</em>, and they, could no longer live in communion. They allege, that <em>Myuru Varmá</em> reinstated them again in the whole property of <em>Tulava</em>.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Actual tenures.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. <em>Jan. 22.</em></aside></div><p class="prose-p">At present, however, the greater part of the country belongs to <em>Bunts</em>, and other <em>Súdras</em>, who style themselves proprietors (<em>Balikies</em>), although the <em>Bráhmans</em> are willing only to give them the title of <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-46" data-page="46" data-folio="32" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n45_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 32">[p. 32]</button> <em>Mulacaras</em>, or tenants. The property, if ever it belonged to the <em>Brahmans</em>, has been entirely alienated; nor is there even a pretence set up, of the <em>Brahmans</em> having a power of redemption.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote"><em>Mulacaras</em>, <em>Balikies</em>, or proprietors.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">The <em>Balikies</em>, <em>Mulacaras</em>, or proprietors, are answerable for the land-tax, called here <em>Shista</em>, and by the Mussulmans <em>Shist</em>. The estate is always called by the <em>Baliki</em> or proprietor’s name, although it is often mortgaged to its full value.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote"><em>Aduvacaras</em>, or mortgagees.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">The mortgagee is here called <em>Aduvacara</em>, from <em>Aduva</em>, a mortgage. The mortgagee pays the amount of the land-tax to the landlord (<em>Baliky</em>), who gives it to government. The remainder of the profit is retained by the mortgagee for the interest of the money that he has advanced, which is in general at the rate of 12 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>2</sub> <em>per cent. per annum</em>: in some places, however, it is only 10 <em>per cent.</em> Land is never mortgaged without a regular writing, wherein is mentioned the sum for which the estate is mortgaged. It may be resumed, by paying up this sum, whenever the landlord pleases; but, if the mortgagee has planted any trees, he must be paid for them at a certain fixed rate, which is known to be equal to the expense that he must have incurred. Many of the landlords retain their own estates, and cultivate much of them with their own stock; but about an eighth of the country has been mortgaged. Some landlords have mortgaged the whole of their estates, and, having had no hopes of being able to redeem them, have entirely left the country. The estates still, however, go by their names, and the tax is paid in their names by the mortgagees.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote"><em>Gaynicaras</em>, or tenants.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">Both proprietors and mortgagees let part of their lands to tenants, or <em>Gaynicaras</em>. In this district, the tenant gives a writing, obliging himself to pay a certain rent, but receives no lease in return; and, whenever the land-holder pleases, may be ejected from his farm. In other districts, however, especially that of <em>Barcuru</em>, the tenant has a lease in perpetuity, of which he can only be deprived by his, or his heirs, failing to pay the stipulated rent. Some of this rent is paid in rice, and some in money.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-47"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="47" data-folio="33" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n46_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 33">[p. 33]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 22. Encouragement given to improvement.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">When a tenant undertakes to plant a garden, he obtains a writing from the landlord, by which he is ensured of the payment of the expenses incurred, should the garden be resumed; and he pays no rent (<em>Gayni</em>) for a number of years sufficient to allow the garden to become productive. The amount of the expenses to be paid is settled by arbitration. When rice-land has been waste, the tenant for two or three years pays nothing, except the tax. This is the account given by the landlords.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The tenants ought, on rice-lands, to have one-half of the produce; so, at least, the proprietors say.
