The proprietors let very few of their gardens, this being a profitable kind of farming.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In this district (<em>Taluc</em>) there are no waste lands; but some fields, actually cultivated, were by Major Monro allowed to be considered as waste, on account of the clamours made by the natives of their poverty.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote"><em>Hindu</em> worship, how supported.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">Although all the <em>Inams</em>, or charity lands, were ordered by <em>Tippoo</em> to be resumed, yet some belonging to temples have been concealed, as is acknowledged both by the <em>Tahsildar</em> and by the <em>Hindu</em> landlords. This has not been disturbed by Major Monro, nor his successor Mr. Ravenshaw; and an allowance is made by the government to both heathen temples and mosques. The principal <em>Hindu</em> temple here receives annually 120 <em>Pagodas</em>, and its lands produce 360, in all 480 <em>Pagodas</em>, or 193<em>l</em>. 8<em>s</em>. 3<em>d</em>. The people are very anxious for its being restored to its former splendour. Major Monro seems to have thought that very moderate expenses should be incurred in supporting the religious ceremonies of the natives, the allowances that he has made for the temples being in general very small. I do not find that this economy has had any bad effect; and it is impossible for a European to be more respected by <em>Hindus</em>, than Major Monro is by those who were lately under his authority.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">All the lands of <em>Tulava</em> are private property, and pay a land-tax.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. <em>Jan.</em> 22.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">In <em>Tulava</em> the state has no lands; the whole is private property. All the land-tax is now paid in money; but before the conquest <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-48" data-page="48" data-folio="34" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n47_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 34">[p. 34]</button> part of it was demanded in rice, and other articles of consumption for the troops, at a low rate, which was fixed by the officers of government. The accompts contain solely the tax which each proprietor ought to pay. When a man alienates part of his lands, he agrees with the purchaser to take a part of the tax, and then the revenue of the new proprietor is entered in the public accompts under his name. The sum which he is to pay is always mentioned in the title deeds; and the government has a right to prevent any division, that is not in proportion to the value of the lands alienated; otherwise the revenue might suffer greatly. The proprietors allege, that the tax amounts to more than the rent, and that they are obliged to borrow money, or to give part of the profit from the lands cultivated with their own stock, to enable them to satisfy the claims of government. Those whom I had assembled to give me information, and most of whom were as fat as pigs, gravely told me, that they were reduced to live upon <em>Kanji</em>, or rice-soup. From what they say, therefore, no estimate can be formed of the share of the rent which they pay to government. Every one thinks himself bound to conceal the truth, and none more so than the native officers of revenue. Every step, indeed, seems to have been taken, by a chaos of weights and measures, and by plausible but false accompts, to keep the state of the country a profound mystery.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Circumstances of the cultivator.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">To judge from appearances, the occupiers of land in <em>Tulava</em> are richer than even those of <em>Malabar</em>, who are, no doubt, in easier circumstances than those in <em>Coimbetore</em>, or those above the <em>Ghats</em>. The universal cry of poverty, however, that prevails in every part of India, and the care, owing to long oppression, with which every thing is concealed, render it very difficult to know the real circumstances of the cultivator. We may safely however conclude, from the violent contest for landed property of every kind in <em>Canara</em>, that each occupant has still a considerable interest in the soil, besides the reward due to him for cultivating whatever his stock enables him to do. It is indeed sincerely to be wished, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-49" data-page="49" data-folio="35" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n48_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 35">[p. 35]</button> that this property may long continue unmolested; as no country can thrive where the absolute property of the soil is vested in the state.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Jan. 22. Farms and stock.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">Cultivators who are rich keep from twenty to twenty-five ploughs, but at least one half of the actual farmers have only one. Those who keep two, three, and four ploughs, are common. Near the sea there are many plantations, and some cultivators take care of these only; but, in general, each cultivator has some rice-ground, and some gardens. In the interior parts of the country very few have gardens. A farmer with four ploughs requires constantly six men, four women, and eight oxen. To transplant his rice, he must also hire women; ten are required to plant in two days a <em>Moray</em> land. The wages of these ten for two days is said to amount to 40 <em>Hanies</em>, or almost the value of the seed; which seems to be exaggerated. A farm, thus stocked, ought to contain 8 <em>Morays</em> sowing. Some people cultivate 10 <em>Morays</em>, but they do it imperfectly. The land, either for rice or pulse, it must be observed, is cultivated twice a year. I made many measurements to endeavour to satisfy myself with respect to the extent of what is called a <em>Moray</em>, or <em>Mudi</em> sowing; but, owing to some artifices of the natives, the results differed so essentially, that I can place no reliance on my own measurements, and am inclined to think the extent very indefinite. The average <em>Moray</em>, according to Mr. Ravenshaw’s answer to my queries, is 1 <sup>13</sup>⁄<sub>1000</sub> acre. At this rate, the eight <em>Morays</em> cultivated by four ploughs would amount to little more than 9 acres, which is absurd. The least that can be allowed for a plough is, I am persuaded, six or seven acres.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Price of labour. <em>Culialu</em>, or hired servants.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. <em>Jan.</em> 22.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">The cultivation is chiefly carried on by <em>Culialu</em>, or hired servants; but there are also some <em>Muladalu</em>, bought men, or slaves. A hired man gets daily 2 <em>Hanies</em> of clean rice, or annually 21 <sup>3</sup>⁄<sub>4</sub> bushels, together with 1 <sup>1</sup>⁄<sub>2</sub> <em>Rupee’s</em> worth of cloth, a <em>Pagoda</em> in cash, and a house. A hired woman gets 1 <sup>1</sup>⁄<sub>2</sub> <em>Rupee</em> for cloth, and <sup>3</sup>⁄<sub>4</sub> of the man’s allowance of grain. In planting season, the women hired by the day get two <em>Hanies</em> of rice, or 128 <sup>1</sup>⁄<sub>4</sub> cubical inches. These wages are very <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-50" data-page="50" data-folio="36" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n49_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 36">[p. 36]</button> high, and may enable the hired servants to keep a family in the greatest abundance. It is evident from hence, that the stock required to cultivate eight <em>Morays</em> of land was excessively exaggerated by the proprietors. The wages, in grain alone, would amount to 156½ <em>Morays</em> of rice for 8 <em>Morays</em> sowing; so that, to pay even them, would require at least 40 seeds. We may safely allow six <em>Morays</em> for each plough fully wrought; but the number of ploughs in the whole district amount to rather less than one to 3 <em>Morays</em> of rice ground in actual cultivation, according to the revenue accompts; owing, probably, to a want of cattle and other stock. At the end of the year, the hired servant may change his service, if he be free from debt; but that is seldom the case. When he gets deeply involved, his master may sell his sisters’ children to discharge the amount, and his services may be transferred to any other man who chooses to take him and pay his debts to his master. In fact, he differs little from a slave, only his allowance is larger, but then the master is not obliged to provide for him in sickness nor in old age.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Slaves.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 22.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">A male slave is allowed daily 1½ <em>Hany</em> of rice, or three-fourths of the allowance for a hired servant; a woman receives one <em>Hany</em>. The man gets 1½ <em>Rupee’s</em> worth of cloth, and 2 <em>Rupees</em> in cash; the woman is allowed only the cloth. They receive also a trifling allowance of oil, salt, and other seasonings. A small allowance is given to children and old people. When a slave wishes to marry, he receives 5 <em>Pagodas</em> (2 guineas) to defray the expense. The wife works with the husband’s master. On the husband’s death, if the wife was a slave, all the children belong to her mother’s master; but, if she was formerly free, she and all her children belong to her husband’s master. A good slave sells for 10 <em>Pagodas</em>, or about 4 guineas. If he has a wife who was formerly free, and two or three children, the value is doubled. The slave may be hired out; and the renter both exacts his labour, and finds him in subsistence. Slaves are also mortgaged; but the mortgager is not obliged to <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-51" data-page="51" data-folio="37" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n50_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 37">[p. 37]</button> supply the place of a slave that dies; and in case of accidents, the debt becomes extinguished; which is an excellent regulation. Free men of low cast, if they are in debt or trouble, sometimes sell their sister’s children, who are their heirs. They have no authority over their own children, who belong to their maternal uncles.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In this country the hill ground is never cultivated, except for gardens; the whole may therefore be divided into rice-land and garden ground.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Rice-land of three kinds.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">The rice land is of three kinds; <em>Bylu</em>, <em>Majelu</em>, and <em>Betta</em>. <em>Bylu</em> ground is that in the lower part of vallies which are watered by small streams, from whence canals are dug to convey the water to the fields, which by this irrigation are able to give annually two crops. The <em>Majelu</em> land is higher than the <em>Bylu</em>, and is provided with small reservoirs, which ensure one crop, even when the rains last only two or three months. From some of these reservoirs, the water is let out by a sluice. It is raised from others by means of the <em>Yatam</em>, or by a basket suspended between ropes. The <em>Betta</em> land is the highest part of the rice ground, and is provided with neither streams nor reservoir; so that the crop depends entirely on the rain. In some places there is another kind of rice ground called <em>Potla</em>. During the rainy season, it is so inundated, that it cannot then be cultivated; and, as the water dries, the rice is transplanted.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote"><em>Bylu</em> rice-land produces three crops annually.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">On the <em>Bylu</em> land there are three crops in the year, 1st. <em>Yenalu</em>, 2d. <em>Sughi</em>, and 3d <em>Colaky</em>. This last is only produced by a few spots particularly favoured with water. The accompanying table will explain several particulars relative to the cultivation of rice.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-52"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="52" data-folio="38" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n51_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 38">[p. 38]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 22.</aside></div><div class="ledger-table-block" id="tbl-009"><div class="table-toolbar"><span class="table-id-badge">TBL-009 · FOLIO 52</span><span class="table-csv-links"><a href="/downloads/journey-from-madras-vol-iii/datasets/tables/tbl-009_p0052.csv" download>Diplomatic CSV</a> · <a href="/downloads/journey-from-madras-vol-iii/datasets/tidy/tbl-009_p0052.tidy.csv" download>Tidy CSV</a></span></div><div class="ledger-table-wrap" role="region" aria-label="Statistical table" tabindex="0"><table class="ledger-table"><caption>Table explaining the Cultivation of Rice at <em>Mangalore</em>.</caption><thead><tr><th scope="col">Kind.</th><th scope="col">Quality.</th><th scope="col">Soil.</th><th scope="col">Crop for which it is used.</th><th scope="col">Months required for this crop.</th><th scope="col">Manner of cultivation.</th><th scope="col">Increase in a good crop. Folds.</th><th scope="col">Produce of an Acre, supposing it to sow 1¼ Bushel.</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr class="row-data"><td><em>Bily Ayki</em></td><td>White and small</td><td><em>Bylu</em></td><td><em>Yenalu</em></td><td class="num">5</td><td>transplanted</td><td class="num">20</td><td class="num">25</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Ditto</td><td>Ditto</td><td>ditto</td><td><em>Colaky</em></td><td class="num">3</td><td>sprouted seed</td><td class="num">5</td><td class="num">6¼</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Ditto</td><td>Ditto</td><td><em>Majelu</em></td><td><em>Yenalu</em></td><td class="num">5</td><td>ditto</td><td class="num">12</td><td class="num">15</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td><em>Jirigay Saly</em></td><td>Very small</td><td><em>Bylu</em></td><td>ditto</td><td class="num">5</td><td>transplanted</td><td class="num">15</td><td class="num">18¾</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td><em>Amutty</em></td><td>Large and black</td><td>ditto</td><td>ditto</td><td class="num">5</td><td>sprouted seed</td><td class="num">20</td><td class="num">25</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td><em>Cagi Ayki</em></td><td>Ditto</td><td>ditto</td><td>ditto</td><td class="num">5</td><td>ditto</td><td class="num">15</td><td class="num">18¾</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Ditto</td><td>Ditto</td><td>ditto</td><td><em>Colaky</em></td><td class="num">3</td><td>ditto</td><td class="num">5</td><td class="num">6¼</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td><em>Atticaráya</em></td><td>Red and low priced</td><td>ditto</td><td><em>Sughi</em></td><td class="num">3½</td><td>ditto</td><td class="num">10</td><td class="num">12½</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td><em>Kiny Vettu</em></td><td>—</td><td><em>Majelu</em></td><td><em>Yenalu</em></td><td class="num">3</td><td>ditto</td><td class="num">10</td><td class="num">12½</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Ditto</td><td>—</td><td><em>Bettu</em></td><td>ditto</td><td class="num">3</td><td>ditto</td><td class="num">8</td><td class="num">10</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td><em>Sampa Saly</em></td><td>—</td><td><em>Majelu</em></td><td>ditto</td><td class="num">3½</td><td>ditto</td><td class="num">10</td><td class="num">12½</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td><em>Soma Saly</em></td><td>—</td><td>ditto</td><td>ditto</td><td class="num">3½</td><td>ditto</td><td class="num">10</td><td class="num">12½</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td>Ditto</td><td>—</td><td><em>Bettu</em></td><td>ditto</td><td class="num">3½</td><td>ditto</td><td class="num">8</td><td class="num">10</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td><em>Tungalu</em></td><td>—</td><td>ditto</td><td>ditto</td><td class="num">3</td><td>ditto</td><td class="num">8</td><td class="num">10</td></tr><tr class="row-data"><td><em>Attigary</em></td><td>—</td><td><em>Potla</em></td><td>—</td><td class="num">5</td><td>transplanted</td><td class="num">10</td><td class="num">12½</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote"><em>Yenalu</em> crop transplanted.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Jan. 22.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">The kinds of rice that are transplanted for the <em>Yenalu</em> crop on <em>Bylu</em> land are cultivated as follows. Between the 14th of May and the 14th of June, water the ground intended for raising the seedlings for two days, and then plough it twice; all the water, except two inches in depth, being let off at each ploughing. The two ploughings must be repeated every other day, until the eighth time. The field, before the last ploughing, is manured with ashes, and with dung, in which, while in the cow-house, the leaves of every kind of bush and tree have been mixed. The mud is then smoothed with the <em>Mutu Pallay</em>, or plank drawn by oxen (Plate XXII. Fig. 58.). The seed, prepared by causing it to sprout, is then sown very thick, the water being three inches deep. Next day the water is let off. On the fifth day, when the shoots come up, they get as much water as covers the half next the ground; and every day, as the plants <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-55" data-page="55" data-folio="39" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n54_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 39">[p. 39]</button> grow, the quantity of water is increased. On the ninth day the water is let entirely off, and is not given again until the eleventh day. If worms affect the plants, about the end of the third week the water is again let off for three days, and some ashes are sprinkled over the field to kill these destructive animals. The seedlings must be transplanted between the 30th and 35th days.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-53"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="53" data-folio="39" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n52_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 39">[p. 39]</button></div><figure class="archival-plate" id="fig-001"><img src="/figures/journey-from-madras-vol-iii/fig-001_p0053.webp" alt="Vol. III. p. 38. — PLATE XXII. — Warner sc." loading="lazy"><figcaption><em>Vol. III. p. 38.</em> — <em>PLATE XXII.</em> — <em>Warner sc.</em></figcaption></figure><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. <em>Jan.</em> 22.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">On the day that the seed is sown, the ground for receiving the seedlings when transplanted begins to be ploughed, and in the course of the month gets four double ploughings. The plough in use here (Plate XXII. Fig. 60.) is neater than usual in India, but is an implement equally wretched. In the intervals between the ploughings, the field is kept inundated. At the time of ploughing, two or three inches only of water are allowed to remain. After every ploughing, the soil is smoothed with the plank drawn by oxen. Between the 4th and 15th of July all the water except one inch is let off, and the seedlings are transplanted. On the third day the field is drained; and for two days it is allowed to dry. On the sixth it receives 2 inches of water, and then is continued inundated until the crop ripens. Between the 5th and 16th of August the weeds are removed by the hand. In October, or at the beginning of November, the straw is cut with the grain, and, till it be dry, is allowed to lie on the ground. In Figure 61, the sickle is delineated. The rice is thrashed by beating handfulls of the straw against a grating of <em>Bamboos</em>, which is placed sloping from a stone to the ground: the grain falls through the grating. This operation is performed in the square surrounded by the farm-houses; for here, as well as in most parts of India, there are no barns. The rough rice is dried in the sun, and much attention is paid to this operation with what is intended for seed. The straw is spread out to the sun as much as possible; but, owing to the rain, is seldom got in well. The seed is kept in <em>Morays</em>, or straw bags, which are hung up in the smoke of the kitchen. The rice intended for consumption is put up in heaps, placed on straw, and covered with thatch. <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-56" data-page="56" data-folio="40" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n55_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 40">[p. 40]</button> The husks are beaten off in the course of two or three months, and immediately sold. The rough rice is put into large pots, overnight, with so much water as will cover it. In the morning it is boiled until the husks begin to open. It is then dried in the sun, and beaten in a small hole in the ground, or in a stone with a long pestle, the end of which is covered with iron. For the use of <em>Bráhmans</em>, a little is beaten without having been boiled; but it does not preserve long.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote"><em>Yenalu</em> crop sown <em>Mola.</em></aside></div><p class="prose-p">The rices that are cultivated as sprouted seed for the <em>Yenalu</em> crop on <em>Bylu</em> land are thus managed. The ploughings and manure are conducted exactly in the same manner as in the field on which the seedlings are raised; but, in order to gain time, they are made fifteen days later. The seed is prepared by putting the <em>Moray</em>, or straw bag, in which it has been kept, into water from the evening until next day at noon. The bag is then removed into the house, and in the morning of the fourth day is opened, the seed is sprinkled with dung and water, and immediately sown. After having been sown, it is managed like the seedlings; but the weeds are removed about the 26th of July. The quantity of seed required on the same ground for the sprouted seed cultivation, is to that required for transplantation, as two to three.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote"><em>Sughi</em> crop.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 22.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">In the <em>Sughi</em> crop on <em>Bylu</em> land the rice is mostly cultivated as sprouted seed. It is inferior in quality to the rice of the <em>Yenalu</em> crop, and is chiefly reserved for home consumption. Being reaped in the hot and dry season, the straw, though short, is well dried, and is a valuable supply of fodder. The sprouted seed for this crop is thus cultivated. Between the 16th of October and the 14th of November, immediately after the <em>Yenalu</em> crop has been reaped, the ploughings commence; and are carried on exactly as before described; only in place of one man’s standing on the plank drawn by oxen, the ground being now harder, three or four men must stand on this instrument; a most barbarous and expensive manner of adding weight; but in India it is seldom that an attempt is made <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-57" data-page="57" data-folio="41" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n56_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 41">[p. 41]</button> to accomplish any thing by machinery, that can be performed by human labour. The quantity of manure required for this crop is larger than that which is given to the first. If this crop be transplanted, it only produces six seeds.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote"><em>Colaky</em> crop of rice.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">The seed of the rices that are cultivated for the <em>Colaky</em> crop is sown sprouted. Between the 12th of January and the 10th of February, immediately after having cut the <em>Sughi</em> crop, the ploughing for the <em>Colaky</em> commences, and the field is managed exactly as in the <em>Sughi</em> crop. In most places the water must be raised by the <em>Yatam</em>, called here the <em>Panay</em>, or by the instrument called <em>Cai</em>-<em>dumbay</em> (Plate XXV. Fig. 62), which makes the cultivation very expensive. The <em>Cai-dumbay</em> cannot raise water more than three feet, and is a means of irrigation very inferior to the basket suspended by ropes and wrought by two men. This crop requires a great deal of manure, otherwise it injures the following crop called <em>Yenalu.</em></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote"><em>Colaky</em> crop of other grams.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">In place of this third crop of rice, where the quantity of water is too small, a crop of <em>Urudu</em> (<em>Phaseolus minimoo</em> Roxb: MSS.), <em>Padingi</em> (<em>Phaseolus Mungo</em>), or <em>Cudu</em> (<em>Dolichos biflorus</em>), is taken from the <em>Bylu</em> land. In some villages, but not in this immediate neighbourhood, a crop of <em>Enama</em> (<em>Sesamum</em>) is taken. For the three leguminous plants the ground in five days gets five double ploughings, and after each is smoothed with the plank drawn by oxen. It is then manured with dung and ashes, and the seed is sown broad-cast, and covered by the plough; after which the soil is again smoothed with the plank drawn by oxen. Then, if the field be not sufficiently moist, it must be divided into small plots surrounded by little banks, and once in fifteen days it must receive water. The quantity of the seed required for these pulses, is one-fourth of that required for rice in the sprouted seed cultivation, or about five-sixteenths of a bushel an acre. The produce is about 8 seeds, or 2½ bushels an acre.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-58"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="58" data-folio="42" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n57_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 42">[p. 42]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 22.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">In order to prevent the torrents of water, which in the rainy season run down from the hills, from injuring the <em>Bylu</em> land, a strong mound is formed round the bottom of the hills; and a channel above this mound conveys all the superfluous water into the sea, or into rivers. Coco-nut trees are frequently planted under the bank, or mound, in order to give it strength.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote"><em>Majelu</em> land.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">All the rices cultivated on the second sort of rice land, called <em>Majelu</em>, are sown sprouted; only, any seedlings, that may happen to remain after planting the <em>Bylu</em> fields are put into the <em>Majelu</em>. The cultivation on this is exactly the same, and at the same season, as the <em>Yenalu</em>, or first crop on <em>Bylu</em> land. The water, in case of a deficiency of rain, is supplied from small tanks, which reserve a supply for fourteen or fifteen days after the rains are over. The seed required for this kind of land is said to be one third more, than that required for the same extent of <em>Bylu</em>; but, on actual measurement, I found that a <em>Moray</em> of seed required considerably more <em>Majelu</em> than it did of <em>Bylu</em>. On a small portion of <em>Majelu</em> land, a second crop of <em>Cudu</em> (<em>Dolichos biflorus</em>) is taken. It is sown between the 16th of October and the 13th of November, and its produce is nearly the same as when cultivated on <em>Bylu</em> land.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote"><em>Betta</em> land rice.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">The third sort of rice land, called <em>Betta</em>, is the same with the lower <em>Parum</em>, or hill-land of <em>Malayala</em>, which is there chiefly used for gardens. The rice cultivated on this is always sown sprouted, exactly in the same manner as the <em>Yenalu</em>, or first crop; only it requires two more ploughings, and a greater quantity of manure. The seed ought to be 1¼ of that which is required for the same extent of <em>Bylu</em>; but this also, I found, was not confirmed by actual measurement. This rice is kept for home consumption; for that of the <em>Yenalu</em>, or first crop from <em>Bylu</em>, or the lowest land, is the kind commonly exported.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Sugar-cane.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 22.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 22.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">It is upon this kind of ground that sugar-cane is cultivated; but very small quantities only are raised, and that entirely by the native <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-59" data-page="59" data-folio="43" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n58_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 43">[p. 43]</button> Christians. Their method is as follows. Between the 14th of December and the 11th of January the ground, for four successive days, has a double ploughing, and, after each, is smoothed with the plank drawn by oxen. Then, with a hoe, called <em>Haray</em> (Plate XXI. Fig. 56), parallel channels are formed, at the distance of every 8 or 10 cubits. At right angles to these, and contiguous to each other, are formed trenches three quarters of a cubit deep, half a cubit wide at the bottom, and one cubit and a half at the top. The field is then manured with dung and straw; which, after they have been spread on the field, are burned; so that, in fact, the manure is ashes. The canes for seed are then cut into pieces, from half to three quarters of a cubit long; and these are soaked in water a whole day and a night. On the day after the manure has been burned on the field, the soil in the bottom of the trenches is loosened with the hoe, and mixed with the ashes; and with these united the joints of the cane are slightly covered. They are placed horizontally, two and two, in lines parallel to the trenches; and the ends of one pair touch the ends of the two adjacent pairs. The field is then watered, the channels being filled from a tank, or well, by means of the machine called <em>Yatam</em>. Except when there is rain, it must be watered every fourth day, speaking as a medical man; that is to say, if it be watered on the 1st day of a month, it will be watered again on the 4th, 7th, 10th, and so forth. A compost having been formed of rich mould, dung, and dry grass, it is burned; and on the 15th day from planting the ashes are spread over the field. At the end of the month, the weeds are removed by the hand, and with a small instrument named <em>Sulingy</em>. At the same time, the young canes are again manured with the burnt compost. At the end of the second month, if the cane has a sickly colour, it is again manured. The rains commence about that time, and then the earth from the intermediate ridges is gathered up round the young canes; which thus, in place of being in trenches, stand on the top of ridges. The field must then be well fenced. <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-60" data-page="60" data-folio="44" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n59_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 44">[p. 44]</button> The dried leaves must be removed by the hand, which is all the farther trouble required, no watering being necessary after the rainy season is over. Jackalls eat the cane, and must be carefully watched. The cane is fit for cutting in 11 or 12 months. There are two kinds; the <em>Bily</em>, and <em>Cari Cabbu</em>; or white, and black canes. The former is the <em>Restali</em>, and the latter the <em>Putta Putty</em> of the country above the <em>Ghats</em>. The same ground will not produce sugar-cane every year; between every two crops of cane there must be two crops of rice. A piece of land that sows one <em>Moray</em> of rice, will produce 4000 canes, which are about six feet long, and sell to the <em>Jagory</em> boilers at from half to one <em>Rupee</em> a hundred. The <em>Moray</em> sowing of <em>Betta</em> land is here about 30,000 square feet; so that, according to the price of sugar cane, the acre produces from about 58 to 29 <em>Rupees</em>, or from about 5<em>l.</em> 17<em>s.</em> to 2<em>l.</em> 18<em>s.</em> 6<em>d.</em> The land-tax is the same as when the field is cultivated for rice. The want of firewood is the greatest obstacle to this cultivation; the <em>trash</em>, or expressed stems, is not sufficient to boil the juice into <em>Jagory</em>, while that operation is performed in earthen pots placed over an open fire. If all the land in <em>Codeal Taluc</em> (district) that is fit for the purpose, were employed to raise sugar-cane, it would yearly produce 1000 <em>Pagodas</em> worth of cane; that is to say, there are about 1125 <em>Mudis</em> sowing of land, that once in three years might be cultivated. The quantity in the neighbouring district on the south side of the river is much greater. The <em>Jagory</em> made here is hard, but black, and of a bad quality. It sells at 3 <em>Maunds</em> for the <em>Pagoda</em>, or at 12<em>s.</em> 3½<em>d.</em> a hundred-weight.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Kitchen-stuffs.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">Between the rows of sugar-cane are raised some cucurbitaceous plants, and some kitchen stuffs, that soon come to maturity.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">On <em>Betta</em>, or the highest of rice-land, where the water may be had by digging to a little depth, some people, chiefly Christians, cultivate capsicum, and <em>Banguns</em> (<em>Solanum Melongena</em>), as a second crop after rice. In good soils, these require to be watered once in three days; in bad soils, they must be allowed water every other day.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-61"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="61" data-folio="45" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n60_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 45">[p. 45]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 22. <em>Potla</em> land.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">The kind of land called <em>Potla</em>, or <em>Mojaru</em>, is situated in deep places near the banks of rivers; and is so much overflowed in the rainy season, that, until the violence of this is over, it cannot be cultivated. Even in the dry season, it would in general be overflowed by the tide at high water; so that it is necessary to make banks to exclude the sea. The rice which it produces is always transplanted. Between the 17th of August and the 15th of September the seed is sown, and is managed in the same manner as the transplanted rice on <em>Bylu</em> land; only the season is different. The same quantity of seed is required for the same extent of <em>Bylu</em> ground; that is, one-half more than would be required for sowing broad-cast. This is a very precarious crop, being subject to be totally ruined by either too little or too much rain.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Poor land of every denomination requires more seed than richer land of the same kind.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Manure.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">The leaves of every kind of tree and bush, except such as are prickly, are used for manure. The cattle are kept in the house all night, and their dung is collected for the same use. It is kept in pits, and every day’s collection is covered with leaves; the whole dunghill thus forming alternate strata of dung and leaves, which soon rot. The ashes and sweepings of the family are kept in a separate pit. The soil of towns is never used as manure.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Palm gardens, soil fit for them.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">In <em>Tulava</em> the coco-nut and <em>Betel-nut</em> are the only productions of the gardens that are taxed. The gardens are formed on hilly ground which has a red soil; but, as the trees require to be watered, such places only are considered fit for the purpose, as afford water by digging wells to no great depth, or as can be watered by forming reservoirs. The water of the wells is raised by the machine called <em>Yatam</em>; but the gardens thus supplied, although requiring a great deal of trouble, are equally valuable with those watered from tanks; for as these sometimes fail in the hot season, the crop for that year is lost, although the trees do not perish.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Cultivation of the <em>Areca</em> palm.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 22.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 22.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">Here the <em>Areca</em> or <em>Betel-nut</em> palm forms separate plantations, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-62" data-page="62" data-folio="46" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n61_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 46">[p. 46]</button> which are surrounded by some rows of the coco-nut tree, and is not scattered about the gardens, as in <em>Malabar</em>. The following is the manner of making one of these plantations, as described by the proprietors. Between the 17th of December, and the 13th of February, the seed must be collected from trees that are at least fifty years old. Having been kept four days in the house, it is tied up in a <em>Moray</em>, or straw-bag, and is immersed for 25 days in the water of a well. In the mean time a small plot of rice ground is repeatedly ploughed until it be reduced to a fine mud, and is well manured with dung and ashes. In this mud the nuts are placed close to one another, with their eyes uppermost, and one half of them above the earth. Then the plot is covered with straw, and is watered once a day for a month. A piece of dry ground is then dug up with the hoe, and manured with dung and ashes. Into this the nuts, which have now sprouted, are transplanted at half a cubit’s distance from each other. The nuts only are covered, and the sprouts are left projecting. For two months, if the soil be moist, it must be watered once in four days; if it be dry, once in three days is sufficient. Another piece of ground is in the mean time prepared; and at the end of the two months the young seedlings are removed thither, and placed at the distance of one cubit from each other. In this nursery they remain eight months; and once in four days, when there is no rain, they are watered. In the mean while the garden is prepared by inclosing it with a dry hedge of prickly bushes. Within the hedge a row of coco-nut palms is planted, each being 24 cubits from the other. Within these, at 10 cubits distance from each other, are formed pits, two cubits in diameter, and two cubits deep. In the bottom of each of these is put a young <em>Areca</em>; all its roots are covered with fine mould, and it is manured with a little dung. This is between the 19th of October and the 16th of November, at the close of the rainy season. Every fourth day the pits must be watered, while the sun is excluded by branches and leaves. At the end of six months some dung must be given, and the weeds <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-63" data-page="63" data-folio="47" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n62_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 47">[p. 47]</button> removed by the hand. Whenever there is no rain the waterings are to be continued; and twice a year the trees must be manured, and the weeds ought to be removed from near their roots. In two years the pits are filled up with the manure. At the end of five years another set of pits is made, one between every two of the old ones; and in these is placed another set of young plants, and managed as the first set. At this second planting some plantain trees (<em>Musas</em>) are set in the garden, but not above forty for the hundred <em>Arecas.</em> Near the hedge, in a line with the coco-nut palms, are also put some <em>Jack</em> (<em>Artocarpus integrifolia</em>) and <em>Mango</em> (<em>Mangifera indica</em>) trees. When ten years old, the <em>Areca</em> begins to produce fruit; but until the fifteenth year does not arrive at perfection. For thirty-five years it continues in full bearing. From its 50th year until its death, which happens in from its 70th to its 100th year, the quantity of fruit gradually diminishes, but its quality rather improves. The trees in full fruit produce annually three bunches, which ripen in succession between the 19th of October and the 16th of December. Each bunch contains from 30 to 100 nuts; so that, according to the natives, 200 nuts may be taken as the average produce of an <em>Areca</em> when it is in vigour. When the <em>Mango</em> and <em>Jack</em> trees have grown up, the pepper vines are usually put round them. Some people plant them also against the <em>Areca</em>, but they diminish its produce. <em>Yams</em> (<em>Dioscoreas</em>) are planted near the hedge.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Manner of collecting and preserving the <em>Betel</em>-<em>nut.</em></aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. <em>Jan.</em> 22.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">The <em>Betel-nut</em> is collected by a set of people called <em>Devadigas</em>, who are sometimes kept as servants, and sometimes hired for the crop season, at 1¼ silver <em>Fanam</em> a day (5½<em>d.</em>), part of which is paid in rice. A <em>Devadiga</em> in the forenoon cuts 25 bunches, and in the afternoon assists the family to prepare the nuts. If the season promise to be favourable, that is to say, not too rainy, when the nuts are three quarters ripe, they are cut for <em>Wan’-Adiky</em>, or <em>dry-betel</em>. Immediately after they are cut, the husk is separated, and the nuts are then put into a pot, with as much water as will cover them, and boiled until the eyes (<em>Corculla</em>) fall out. They are then cut <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-64" data-page="64" data-folio="48" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n63_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 48">[p. 48]</button> into eight pieces, and dried in the sun four days, being removed into the house at night, or on the appearance of rain. It is of great advantage to the <em>Betel</em> to be dried on a gray granite rock (<em>Bily Cullu</em>); but where that cannot be procured, it is dried on a piece of ground that is purposely made hard and smooth. For this operation, the <em>Devadiga</em> requires the assistance of four people, generally the women of the house; and they prepare daily 12 <em>Seers</em> measure of <em>Wan’-Adiky</em> (1 <sup>655</sup>/<sub>1000</sub> peck). When the weather threatens to be rainy, the nuts are allowed to ripen on the tree for <em>Nir’-Adiky</em>, or <em>wet-betel</em>, which is thus prepared. The nuts, with the husk on, just as they are taken from the bunch, are put into large jars full of water, and the mouths of these are closely shut. In this state they cannot be preserved longer than four or five months, and are therefore taken for immediate consumption. A quantity adequate to supply the demand is daily taken out of the jar, and skinned as wanted. The knives used in preparing <em>Betel-nut</em> are delineated in Plate XXII. Fig. 63, 64.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Expense of cultivation.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">A garden of 300 <em>Arecas</em>, which is one of a middling size, if it be watered by a well, requires the labour of six people, but of three only if it be watered by a tank. In the rainy season, however, while the cultivation of rice is chiefly carried on, the three men who are employed to raise the water have nothing to do in the garden, and are employed on the rice ground; even the three other men may be a few hours daily employed at any other kind of work. In fact, I suspect that the men, who spoke of six servants and four ploughs being requisite to cultivate 8 <em>Morays</em> of rice-land, ought to have added to the account an <em>Areca</em> garden of 300 trees. These men get 1 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>2</sub> <em>Pagoda</em> a year in money, 2 <em>Rupees</em> worth of cloth, and eat three times a day in their master’s house.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Black pepper.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 22.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">The pepper is managed as follows. Between the 24th of May and the 22d of June, the ground near the tree upon which it is to be trained is dug with a hoe. Then two, three, or four cuttings of the pepper vine, each a cubit long, are put in the ground, one end <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-65" data-page="65" data-folio="49" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n64_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 49">[p. 49]</button> them being allowed to project. They are then covered with grass. This is done when the rainy season commences. A month afterwards they get a little dung. As the vines shoot, they are tied to the tree. When the dry season commences, they must be watered every second day, until a year old, after which they require water once in four days. Twice a year also they must get manure of dung and leaves; and long grass, or bushes, must be prevented from growing near their roots; but there is no occasion to dig or plough the whole ground. They begin to bear in the fifth year; but are not in full crop until the eighth. If the worms attack the vine, they die in twelve or fifteen years; but otherwise they live twenty-five, and all the while produce good crops. When any vine dies, a new one is planted in its stead. Here they are trained upon the <em>Pongary</em> or <em>Hongary</em> (<em>Erythrina</em>), the <em>Nuriga</em> (<em>Moringa</em>), <em>Jack</em> (<em>Artocarpus</em>), <em>Mango</em> (<em>Mangifera</em>), <em>Areca</em>, coco-nut, and tamarind. The first is, however, most commonly employed, and in this country lives fifty years. It is not customary here to prune the trees upon which the pepper is trained. Each tree, according to the number of vines that it can support, produces from two to four <em>Pucka Seers</em> measure, or from <sup>5483</sup>/<sub>10000</sub> parts to 1,<sup>0966</sup>/<sub>10000</sub> of a Winchester gallon, which will weigh from 2,<sup>625</sup>/<sub>10000</sub> lb. to 5,<sup>25</sup>/<sub>100</sub> lb. When one or two berries begin to appear red, the whole are collected by pinching off the <em>amenta</em>. A man, in one day, can take the fruit from three trees, that is to say, can cure about 12 pounds of pepper. It is kept all night in the house. Next day the berries are rubbed off with the hands, and picked clean. They are then dried three days on mats, or on a piece of smooth hard ground, and every night are taken into the house. The pepper is then fit for sale, and the common price is one <em>Vir’-Ráya Fanam</em> for the <em>Seer</em>, which is at the rate of 106 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>2</sub> <em>Rupees</em> a <em>Candy</em> of 560 lb. the weight here in use; or at the rate of 120 <em>Rupees</em> nearly for the <em>Candy</em> of 640 lb. which the cultivators in <em>Malabar</em> employ. The export price is on an average 136 <em>Rupees</em> <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-66" data-page="66" data-folio="50" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n65_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 50">[p. 50]</button> for the small <em>Candy</em>; but in this the merchants profit and the customs are included.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Jan. 22. Sale of black pepper.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">The crop season is between the 15th of January and the 13th of February. Some people take advances; but the practice does not seem to be so prevalent as in <em>Malabar</em>, and the terms are somewhat more reasonable, although abundantly severe on the imprudent cultivator. If the advance be made six months before the time of delivery, the borrower gets three fourths of the value of the pepper; so that the lender has a profit of one <em>Rupee</em> for every three advanced, or 33⅓ <em>per cent</em>. If, however, there is a delivery short of the stipulated quantity, the merchant gets back only a proportional part of the advance, with interest at the rate of three fourths of a <em>Rupee</em> for the <em>Pagoda per annum</em>, that is to say, 18¾ <em>per cent</em>.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Coco-nut plantations.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">Although I examined both the cultivators and extractors of palm wine concerning the plantations of coco-nut trees, the account that I can give of them is not at all satisfactory; what they said being in some places evidently false, and in others contradictory.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Account of them by the proprietors who cultivate their own gardens.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 22.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">The cultivators say, that the seed must be allowed one whole year on the tree to ripen, and must be the produce of a palm above fifty years old. After being plucked, it is kept four months in a place which is sheltered from the sun and rain. Then it is put in a well, and kept a month under water. A small plot of dry ground is then dug, and manured with dung and ashes. In this the coco-nuts are placed, at one cubit’s distance from each other, and buried so as just to be covered above the eyes, which are placed uppermost. The plot must be near a tank or rivulet, from which with a wooden scoop, <em>Tay-pallay</em> (Plate XXV. Fig. 68.), the water is thrown into it every other day when there is no rain. If there be rain, pains must be taken to prevent too much from lodging on the plot. These operations may be performed at any season; so that the young plants, after remaining in the plot from 12 to 15 months, may be fit for transplanting between the 22d of July and the 20th of August. In <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-67" data-page="67" data-folio="51" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n66_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 51">[p. 51]</button> this month square pits two cubits in width, two cubits deep, and at 24 cubits distance, are dug; and in the bottom of each is placed a coco-nut with its young shoot, which then is about three feet high. Round it are placed a <em>Seer</em> of salt, some ashes, and as much fine mould as will rise four inches above the nut and roots. The young plant must be watered every other day, until the second leaves expand, which will be in about six weeks. In dry weather they must, for at least five years, be watered once in four days. In low grounds near the sea or inlets, the trees after this age require no watering: but on high ground, during the dry season, they must be watered as long as they live. In both situations the trees must be manured twice a year with ashes, dung, and leaves; and, if at a distance from the sea-water, they must at the same time get a little salt. When the first set are from five to ten years old, another set is planted in the spaces between them. They arrive at full perfection in twelve years, and continue in vigour until sixty. Those in plantations near the sea die at this age. These require no trouble; but after five years of age to be manured once in six months; and here no plantation is hoed or ploughed. Every second year, in the rainy season, between the 24th of May and the 16th of November, those trees which grow in low places near the sea are let for six months to the people who extract the juice. During this time, owing to the quantity of rain, the nuts in such situations do not ripen. In the year in which juice is extracted, the tree gives four bunches of nuts; in the intermediate year it gives six bunches. According to the farmers, a garden on high ground, that contains 500 trees, if watered by a tank, requires twenty men to work it; if watered by a well, it requires thirty men in the rainy, and forty in the dry season. This, however, must be an excessive exaggeration. In the dry season these trees may once in three years be let for extracting juice; but the practice is not common. Each tree, while in vigour, ought annually to produce fifty nuts. Those on the low ground produce more, but on the high-land they live much longer. <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-68" data-page="68" data-folio="52" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n67_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 52">[p. 52]</button> They there continue in full vigour until sixty years old, and for about ninety more gradually decay.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Jan. 22. Account given by the <em>Biluaras</em>, who extract the juice.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">The men who extract the juice in general hire the trees when these are fit for their purpose. The rate that they give seems very low, being only one fourth of a <em>Rupee</em> for three trees near the salt-water, and one fourth of a <em>Rupee</em> for four or five trees growing on hill-land; and there must be some mistake, as both to the north and south the rate for each tree is half a <em>Rupee</em>. It is true, that here the trees are never exhausted, and, even in the year in which juice is taken, produce a crop of nuts. According to the <em>Biluaras</em> the trees near the sea can at all times yield juice, those growing on hills produce it only in the rainy season; which is directly contrary to the assertion of the cultivators. The juice is partly sold, for drink, while fermenting; partly distilled into a liquor called <em>Gungasir</em>; and partly boiled into <em>Jagory</em>.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Customs of this cast.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 22.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">The people who follow the business of extracting juice from palm trees, in their native language of <em>Tulava</em>, are called <em>Biluaras</em>; but in that of <em>Karnáta</em>, which the people of rank here commonly use, they are called <em>Halépeca Davaru</em>. Their proper business is to extract juice from palm trees, to boil it down to <em>Jagory</em>, or to distil it into spirituous liquor; but many of them also cultivate the ground, a few as masters, but many more as <em>Culialu</em>, or hired servants. Some of this cast have now settled above the <em>Ghats</em>. These will marry the daughters of the people remaining in <em>Tulava</em>; but those here will not marry a girl from <em>Karnáta</em>, because the property there goes to a man’s children, but here it goes to the children of his sisters; and, if he married a girl from <em>Karnáta</em>, her brothers would not receive the children. The <em>Biluaras</em> pretend to be <em>Súdras</em>, but acknowledge their inferiority to the <em>Bunts</em>. The business of the cast is settled by a person called <em>Guricara</em>, who is appointed for the purpose by the government, and who, with the assistance of a council of elders, has the power of excommunication, and of inflicting corporal punishment. None of this cast can read. They are permitted <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-69" data-page="69" data-folio="53" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n68_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 53">[p. 53]</button> to eat animal food, but ought not to drink intoxicating liquor. The men are allowed a plurality of women, who live in their houses; but on the husband’s death the widows, with their children, return to their brother’s houses, and the eldest son of the eldest sister of the deceased person becomes master of his house and property. If a man fall into poverty, his children go to their uncle’s house, before their father’s death. Girls continue to be marriageable after the age of puberty; and a widow, or divorced woman, may marry again. A man may turn away his wife when he pleases; but a woman cannot leave her husband without his consent. This however, by committing adultery with any person of the cast, she can in general procure; for few husbands retain their wives when unfaithful; and she is not disgraced, but may get another husband, or at any rate she can live with her brother. Those who are in easy circumstances burn their dead; those who die poor are buried. The spirits of good men are supposed to go to a heaven called <em>Sorgum</em>, those of bad men are sent to a place of punishment called <em>Nuraka</em>. They seem to have no idea of transmigration. A few of them worship <em>Vishnu</em>; the greater part, however, never pray to any of the great gods, but content themselves with an annual sacrifice to <em>Marima</em>, and the other <em>Saktis</em>, by which they hope to avert the evils that are occasioned by these agents of <em>Siva</em>. Their women are liable to disorders that are attributed to the influence of <em>Paisáchi</em>, or evil spirits. These are not appeased by sacrifices; but the <em>Biluaras</em> apply to the <em>Cunian</em>, whose <em>Mantrams</em>, they fancy, are capable of casting out these devils. None of the <em>Biluaras</em> have <em>Puróhitas</em> to read <em>Mantrams</em> or <em>Sástrams</em> on occasion of any ceremony, such as marriage, or the commemoration of their deceased parents; nor have those who confine their worship to the <em>Saktis</em> any <em>Guru</em>; but those who pray to <em>Vishnu</em> are subject to the <em>Sri Vaishnavam Bráhmans</em>, who accept of their <em>Dharma</em>, or duty, and bestow on them <em>Upadésa</em>, <em>Chakrántikam</em>, holy-water, and the like.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-70"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="70" data-folio="54" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n69_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 54">[p. 54]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 22. Account of the coco-nut plantations by the tenants.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">But to return to the gardens. The tenants (<em>Gaynigaras</em>) not only differ from the <em>Biluaras</em>, but also give a different account from the proprietors (<em>Mulucaras</em>). They say, that when they are disposed to plant a garden, they agree with a proprietor for a piece of ground suited to the purpose. They agree to give him a fixed annual rent in money; and so long as they pay this, the garden cannot on any pretence be resumed. In case of a deficiency of rent, the proprietor may resume the garden; but he must pay the tenant for all improvements made by planting. The value of each kind of tree is fixed, and is not left to arbitration, as was alleged by the proprietors. For coco-nut palms the value differs, according to their age, from one to three <em>Rupees</em>. A <em>Betel-nut</em> palm is valued at one fourth of a <em>Rupee</em>; ten or twelve fruit trees at one <em>Rupee</em>; a tree covered with pepper vines one <em>Rupee</em>. The expense of rearing all these must be as great here as in <em>Malabar</em>; and we may safely conclude, that these values at least equal the expense incurred. A tenant cannot sell his garden; but he may at any time go to the proprietor and compel him to take it off his hands, and to pay the value of the trees. The tenants sometimes hire gardens that have been brought to maturity. In this case, they pay a certain sum for each palm, but nothing for any of the other articles that are reared in the garden. The proprietor continues to cultivate the garden, and to keep up the number of the trees. This seems to be a reason for the low state at which the cultivation of pepper is in <em>Tulava</em>; as the proprietor is not at all interested in increasing the number of vines.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote"><em>Betel-leaf.</em></aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 22.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 22.</aside></div><p class="prose-p"><em>Betel-leaf</em> (<em>Piper Betle</em>) is here cultivated in separate gardens, as is the case in most parts of India, except in <em>Malabar</em>. For this purpose, a red stony soil on the side of a rising ground is preferred. Some of the gardens are watered from tanks; others, by means of the <em>Yatam</em>, from wells, in which the water stands from 12 to 24 feet under the surface. Between the 23d of April and the 23d of May the ground is first dug, and is then formed into beds six cubits <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-71" data-page="71" data-folio="55" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n70_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 55">[p. 55]</button> wide, which are separated by trenches three fourths of a cubit broad, and half a cubit deep. In the centre of each trench, at four finger-breadths from each other, are planted, in a row, cuttings of the <em>Betel-vine</em>, each a cubit in length. If there is no rain, they must be slightly watered five times a day, and then covered with branches to keep off the sun. At the end of the first and second months, a little fresh red soil, mixed with small stones, are put in the bottoms of the trenches. At the end of the third month a row of branches, at six or eight cubits from each other, is planted on each side of every trench. The branches are intended to grow up to trees as supports to the vines. Those chosen are the <em>Pongary</em> (<em>Erythrina</em>), the <em>Nuriga</em> (<em>Moringa</em>), and the <em>Agashay</em> (<em>Æschynomene grandiflora</em>). At the same time, a little more earth and some dung are put into the trenches. In the sixth month more earth and dung is given; and, <em>Bamboos</em> having been tied horizontally along the rows of branches, the young <em>Betel-vines</em> are tied up to these. At the same time, in the middle of every second bed, a channel is formed, which every other day is filled with water; and from thence, by means of the <em>Tay-pallay</em> (Plate XXV. Fig. 68 ), the water must be thrown on the plants. Every month, a little dung and red earth is put to the roots of the vines, and these are tied up to the <em>Bamboos</em> and trees. When a year old, the garden begins to produce leaves for sale; after which, once in two months, it requires to be manured, and in dry weather to be watered once in two days. In the centre of each of the beds that have no channels, is then put a row of plantain trees. The garden is generally surrounded by a quickset hedge, at other times by a dead hedge of prickly bushes, and in the interval between the fence and vines are planted <em>Capsicums</em>, and other kitchen stuffs. Every four years the <em>Betel-vines</em> die; but in their stead others are immediately planted, a new trench being dug in the situation of each old one. In eighteen or twenty years, the soil having been exhausted, all that is near the trees is removed, and in its place fresh red earth is brought into the garden. <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-72" data-page="72" data-folio="56" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n71_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 56">[p. 56]</button> The trees last for fifty or sixty years; but when, by accident, one dies sooner, a fresh branch is planted to supply its loss. These substitutes, however, do not thrive. When, from old age, the whole trees begin to decay, the garden is abandoned, and a new one is formed in another place. If the garden receive its supply of water from a reservoir, the cultivator, each time that he plants, pays to the proprietor 10 gold <em>Fanams</em>, or 2½ <em>Rupees</em> for every 1000 vines. In the three intermediate years he pays nothing. If the water be supplied from a well, the rent is only half of the above mentioned sum.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Cattle and fodder.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 22.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">The cattle employed in labour here are chiefly bred in the inland districts about <em>Subhra-mani</em>, and are no larger than those of <em>Malabar</em>. From the month of January, until the commencement of the rainy season, they are supported on fodder. Between the 17th of November and the 16th of December a bad hay is made of the long grass which grows naturally on some hills that are purposely kept clear of bushes. This hay is chopped, and is boiled with rice husks for three hours; of this the oxen are allowed a quantity morning and evening; half a <em>Maund</em> (14 lb.), the people say, would be a good allowance. At night they get rice straw to the amount of about three fourths of a <em>Maund</em> (21 lb.), as the people whom I consulted conjecture; but, from the appearance of the cattle, the quantity allowed cannot be near so much. The people indeed merely spoke by guess, no <em>Hindu</em>, so far as ever I heard, having thought of weighing fodder. At the end of the dry season the cattle, as usual in India, become very poor; but in the rainy season those here are fat, and the cows are entirely supported by pasturing on the hills: at night the working cattle are allowed rice straw. An ox is wrought from sun rise until noon only, and is allowed the afternoon to pasture. Epidemic diseases are sometimes very destructive, and are attributed to a contagion which is supposed always to originate above the <em>Ghats</em>. An old man says, that he remembers twenty times the prevalence of this epidemic; but that seems to be speaking in <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-73" data-page="73" data-folio="57" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n72_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 57">[p. 57]</button> round numbers: for the five last years there has been no disease of the kind. A good cow gives twice a day half a <em>Seer</em> of milk. For this purpose few female buffaloes are kept, but a great many males are employed in the plough. Swine are kept by some of the low casts; but the pork of tame swine is an abomination with the <em>Bunts</em>, as with all the higher ranks of <em>Hindus</em>, although many of them are fond of the meat of the wild hog. No horses, sheep, goats, nor asses are bred in <em>Tulava</em>; nor have its inhabitants any carts.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Salt.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 22.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">Salt is made on this coast by a process similar to that used in <em>Malabar</em>; but the quantity manufactured is very inadequate to the demand of the country. A low piece of ground covered by the flood, but dry at low water, is chosen, and surrounded by a bank that is capable of excluding the tide. By means of a tunnel passing through the bank, and formed of a hollow coco-nut tree, the salt water can at pleasure be admitted. A sufficient quantity having been received, the tunnel is shut; and, when the water has evaporated, the soil is very strongly impregnated with salt. Brine is formed, as usual in India, by filtering salt water through this saline earth. The brine is exposed to the sun in small plots, levelled, and rendered impenetrable to water by a coating of clay and sand well beaten together, and rubbed smooth with a stone. To form the salt requires 28 hours evaporation; and it can be made only between the 26th of March and the 23d of May. The man who makes it gets from the government an advance of five <em>Pagodas</em> in cash, and of rice to the same amount. He repays the money, but not the rice, and pays on an average a tax of 43 <em>Pagodas</em>; so that, in fact, government gets from him 38 <em>Pagodas</em> (15<em>l</em>. 16<em>s</em>. 3<em>d</em>.) for an ordinary salt-field. Larger or smaller ones pay in proportion. The manufacturer sells his salt as he pleases. It is mixed with a considerable quantity of earthy impurities, but not with more than the common salt of <em>Bengal</em> contains. The grains are large and cubical, and often adhere together in large porous masses. It seems to be <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-74" data-page="74" data-folio="58" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n73_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 58">[p. 58]</button> very deliquescent. The common price is 1120 <em>Seers</em> for the <em>Pagoda</em>. The <em>Seer</em> measures 76½ cubical inches; the bushel therefore, in cluding the duties, costs less than 2½<em>d.</em></p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">No mines.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">No iron is made in the province of <em>Canara</em>.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Commerce.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">Having assembled the principal traders of this place, they say, not only that the trade of the place has decayed greatly since the time of <em>Hyder</em>, which may possibly be true; but they also assert, contrary to the evidence of the custom-house accompts, that since the fall of <em>Tippoo</em> the imports have diminished greatly. They acknowledge, however, that under this prince the merchants suffered terrible oppressions, and that under his government the greater part of them were ruined. <em>Hyder</em> had collected them together with great pains, and he always allowed a <em>Lac</em> of <em>Rupees</em> (10,073<em>l.</em> 12<em>s.</em> 2½<em>d.</em>) to be in advance to honest and industrious, but poor men; by which means such valuable persons were induced to come from great distances, and to settle at this place. The principal merchants in <em>Hyder’s</em> time were <em>Moplays</em> and <em>Kankánies</em>; a few came from <em>Guzzerat</em>. Since the Company has acquired the government of the country, many men of substance have come from <em>Surat</em>, <em>Cutch</em>, <em>Bombay</em>, and other places to the north. These men are chiefly of the <em>Vaisya</em> cast, but a good many <em>Parsis</em> are among them. The shopkeepers are still mostly <em>Moplays</em> and <em>Kankánies</em>. The <em>Bunts</em> are now beginning to pursue commerce. The vessels employed in trade chiefly belong to other ports.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Exports.</aside><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 22.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">Rice is the grand article of export. It is sent to <em>Muscat</em>, <em>Bombay</em>, <em>Goa</em>, and <em>Malabar</em>. The duties on its exportation were lowered by Major Monro; but that has made no material difference in the price, and the cultivators are not sensible of any benefit from this measure. The average price, including duties and shipping charges, varies, according to its quantity, from 24 to 18½ <em>Pagodas</em> a <em>Corge</em> of 42 <em>Morays</em>. This makes the price from almost 3<em>s.</em> 6½<em>d.</em> to 2<em>s.</em> 8¾<em>d.</em> a bushel. The cultivators, of course, sell it lower; about 2 <em>Morays</em> <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-75" data-page="75" data-folio="59" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n74_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 59">[p. 59]</button> for a <em>Pagoda</em> may be the average price that they get for good rice, which is 3<em>s</em>. 1<em>d</em>. a bushel. The coarser kinds are lower in proportion.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Next to rice, <em>Supári</em> or <em>Betel-nut</em> is the chief export. It is sent to <em>Surat</em>, <em>Bombay</em>, and <em>Cutch</em>. The export price of the raw nut is 14 <em>Pagodas</em> a <em>Candy</em>, or 1<em>l</em>. 2<em>s</em>. 4½<em>d</em>. a hundred-weight. That of the boiled nut is 15 <em>Pagodas</em>, or 1<em>l</em>. 3<em>s</em>. 11¾<em>d</em>. a hundred-weight.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Black-pepper the merchants reckon the next greatest article of export; but, to judge from the custom-house accompts, it would seem to be more considerable. Its average price is 34 <em>Pagodas</em> a <em>Candy</em>, or 3<em>l</em>. 1<em>s</em>. 1<em>d</em>. a hundred-weight. The customs on pepper are lower here than in <em>Malabar</em>, and no rent nor tax is exacted from the cultivator; yet the price at <em>Mangalore</em> is higher than at <em>Tellichery</em>, and the cultivation is more neglected.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Sandal wood is sent to <em>Bombay</em>; but it is all the produce of the country above the <em>Ghats</em>.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Cassia</em>, called here <em>Dhál’-China</em>, or cinnamon, is sent to <em>Muscat</em>, <em>Cutch</em>, <em>Surat</em>, and <em>Bombay</em>; and is exported at 9 <em>Pagodas</em> the <em>Candy</em>, or 14<em>s</em>. 4½<em>d</em>. the hundred-weight. The buds of this tree are called <em>Cabob China</em>, which seems to be the origin of the European word <em>Cubeb</em>. They are exported to the same places.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Turmeric grows in the country, and is exported to <em>Muscat</em>, <em>Cutch</em>, <em>Surat</em>, and <em>Bombay</em>, at the rate of 8 <em>Pagodas</em> a <em>Candy</em>, or 12<em>s</em>. 9½<em>d</em>. a hundred-weight.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">Imports.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">The chief imports, according to these merchants, are blue cotton cloths from <em>Surat</em>, <em>Cutch</em>, and <em>Madras</em>. The <em>Surat</em> cloth is the most common. It is 36 cubits long, two broad, and of a very dark colour, and sells for from 18 to 50 <em>Pagodas</em> a <em>Corge</em>, or from 3⅘ to 10 <em>Rupees</em> a piece.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Coarse white cotton cloth from <em>Cutch</em>, <em>Bavanagur</em>, and other places north from <em>Bombay</em>.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Salt from <em>Bombay</em> and <em>Goa</em>. The former sells at 70 <em>Pagodas</em> a <em>Cumbu</em>, and the latter at 50 <em>Pagodas</em>: the former is a little more than 3¼<em>d</em>. and the latter than 2½<em>d</em>. a bushel.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-76"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="76" data-folio="60" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/journeyfrommadra03hami_2/page/n75_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 60">[p. 60]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><div class="sidenote-stack"><aside class="shoulder-sidenote">CHAPTER XIV. Jan. 22.</aside></div><p class="prose-p">Raw-silk, for the use of the manufacturers above the <em>Ghats</em>, is imported from <em>China</em> and <em>Bengal</em>; and from <em>Muscat</em> a kind of red dye, called <em>Munjisht</em>, which I believe is a species of madder.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Sugar is imported from <em>Bengal</em> and <em>China</em>, and oil and <em>Ghee</em> (boiled butter) from <em>Surat</em>.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Much of the cloth used in the country is brought from above the <em>Ghats</em>; partly by the merchants of this place, and partly by those of <em>Bangalore</em> and <em>Cuddapa</em>.</p></div>
